In an interior chart take C = 0. Radius-r rescaling multiplies face curvature by r, smooth-side
curvature by r2 , and the forcing by r/M1 if gradients are divided by M1 . Thus the additional
bounded flux is small under the stated small-error rescaling. The construction also applies to balls
centered near the reflected face.

                                                     32
Gradient drop or affine entry. Fix r∗ ∈ (0, 1/32) and b0 > 0. There exist k∗ ∈ (r∗ , 1) and a
small error threshold such that a normalized solution on B1 , with |∇z|g ≤ 1, satisfies either

                              sup |∇z|g ≤ k∗ ,                                                   (5.8)
                              Br∗
                                                                       1
                             ∥z − L∥L∞ (Br∗ ) ≤ b0 r∗ ,                2 ≤ |DL| ≤ 2              (5.9)

for some affine L. Small errors comprise forcing, metric and connection errors, smooth-side curvature,
and reflected-face curvature. Both k∗ and the threshold may depend on b0 .
    Otherwise choose errors tending to zero, with the gradient supremum on Br∗ tending to one
and without affine entry. Normalize zj (0) = 0. Equation (5.3) gives uniform local W 2,2 bounds and
hence uniform local L2 bounds on Dsj , where sj = 1 − |∇zj |2gj . Equation (5.7) reads

                     div(aj Dsj + Bj ) ≤ −κ|D2 zj |2 + ϵj ,                 ∥Bj ∥∞ + ϵj −→ 0

with uniform ellipticity and κ > 0. Weak Harnack, after discarding the Hessian term, gives sj → 0
in measure on B1/2 , since inf Br∗ sj → 0.
    For completeness, convergence of Dsj needs a separate test. With a cutoff ζ supported in B1/2 ,
test the inequality by ζ 2 (b − sj )+ , 0 < b < 1. Ellipticity and Young give
                        ∫                        (       ∫              ∫
                                    2       2        2             2
                                   ζ |Dsj | ≤ C b             |Dζ| +        ζ 2 |Bj |2
                         {sj <b}
                                                             ∫                       ∫    )
                                                     +b          ζ|Dζ||Bj | + ϵj b       ζ2 .   (5.10)

Its limsup on a smaller compact ball is at most Cb2 . On {sj ≥ b}, measure convergence and the
uniform L2 gradient bound make the L1 norm of Dsj tend to zero. On {sj < b}, the limsup is at
most Cb. Sending b to zero gives local L1 convergence of Dsj . A fixed cutoff in the full deficit
inequality now yields D2 zj → 0 in L2 (B1/3 ). Poincaré makes the gradients converge to a constant
vector, of length one because sj → 0. Uniform Lipschitz control gives uniform convergence on
smaller balls to the corresponding affine function, a contradiction. Here and below we use the usual
inhomogeneous weak Harnack and scalar divergence estimates [13].

Improvement near a nonzero affine function. There are α2 > 0, λ ∈ (0, 1/8), and sufficiently
small b0 , δ ′ > 0 with this property: if
                                                                       1
                            ∥z − L∥L∞ (B1 ) ≤ b ≤ b0 ,                 4 ≤ |DL| ≤ 4,

the gradient is uniformly bounded, and metric, connection and forcing errors are at most bδ ′ , then
an affine L′ satisfies
                         ∥z − L′ ∥L∞ (Bλ ) ≤ bλ1+α2 , |DL′ − DL| ≤ Cb.                        (5.11)
Indeed Y = (z − L)/b has bounded supremum and bounded local W 2,s0 norm by (5.3). The
normalized connection term is small because it uses the original gradient bound before division by
b. As b0 , δ ′ → 0, Dz − DL = bDY → 0 locally in measure. Freeze only the axis at e0 = DL/|DL|,
leaving χ measurable. Bounded coefficient differences converging in measure, and the Ls0 Hessian
bound, give
                                 ∥∆e⊥ Y + χ∂e20 Y ∥L2 (B3/4 ) −→ 0.                          (5.12)
                                        0




                                                         33
The Dirichlet Laplace inverse on the convex ball B3/4 has ∥D2 v∥2 ≤ ∥∆v∥2 , by integration by parts.
Thus the frozen operator has a zero-Dirichlet inverse in W 2,2 by a contraction of norm at most 1 − c.
Subtract the resulting small correction to obtain Y0 , uniformly bounded in W 2,2 , with

                                             ∆e⊥ Y0 + χ∂e20 Y0 = 0.
                                                0


   The derivative ζ0 = ∂e0 Y0 ∈ W 1,2 satisfies

                                 div [(I − e0 ⊗ e0 + χe0 ⊗ e0 )Dζ0 ] = 0                             (5.13)

in distributions. Differentiation acts on the L2 flux χ∂e0 ζ0 ; no classical derivative of χ is being
asserted. Uniform scalar divergence estimates give ζ0 ∈ C α1 for some α1 ∈ (0, 1). Caccioppoli after
subtraction of a local value gives
                                         ∫
                                                |Dζ0 |2 ≤ Crn−2+2α1 .
                                           Br

The source in ∆Y0 = (1 − χ)∂e0 ζ0 therefore has L1 mass at most Crn−1+α1 . The gradient of its
localized Newton potential is C α1 : for points separated by a, near annuli of radius R ≤ a contribute
CRα1 , and the far-field gradient difference contributes CaRα1 −1 for R ≥ a. Both dyadic sums
converge. Subtracting this potential leaves a harmonic function on a smaller ball. All first derivatives
of Y0 thus have a uniform Hölder bound.
    The unfrozen Y has a uniform interior Hölder modulus by the scalar Krylov–Safonov estimate
with bounded forcing [13, Section 9.8]; see also [19] for the homogeneous mechanism. The L2 -small
correction Y − Y0 is consequently small uniformly on a smaller ball, using the uniform moduli of
both terms. Taylor approximation of Y0 , first choosing 0 < α2 < α1 and small λ, and then b0 , δ ′ ,
proves (5.11).

Iteration and Hessian growth. At each center begin with a fixed gradient normalization and
sufficiently small radius for both error thresholds. On each gradient drop reduce the radius by r∗
and the normalization by k∗ . Since r∗ /k∗ < 1, normalized forcing stays small; geometric errors
also decrease. If affine entry never occurs, the approximation by a constant has order r1+αd , where
αd = log k∗ / log r∗ > 0.
    After affine entry rescale its smaller ball to unit size and keep the same gradient normalization.
Iterate (5.11) at radii λi , with errors bi = b0 λiα2 . Only constants, not affine functions, are subtracted
when rescaling the structural equation, so its axis remains that of the actual gradient. Forcing and
geometric errors decrease like λi , faster than bi . The slope increments Cbi are summable; small b0
keeps slopes in [1/4, 4]. Combining the two regimes gives uniform affine approximation of order
r1+α , also across the entry scale, for α ≤ min(αd , α2 ). The affine Campanato criterion gives the
gradient Hölder estimate. The same iteration applies in corrected reflected charts.
    Finally subtract the first affine jet in (5.3). Its remainder is O(r1+α ), and the forcing and
connection terms are bounded. The squared s = 2 estimate gives
                                     ∫
                                           |D2 z|2 ≤ C(rn + rn−2+2α ),
                                      Br

which implies (5.2), with equivalent covariant norms and the same argument on half-balls.




                                                       34
5.2   The second equation and coupled regularity
Lemma 5.2 (A bounded solution with natural gradient growth). On a ball suppose a bounded,
individually smooth function Z solves

                                     − div(A DZ) = div B + F,

where A is symmetric, bounded and uniformly elliptic, B is bounded, and

                          |F | ≤ a0 |DZ|2 + µ,      µ(Br (x)) ≤ C0 rn−2+α0

for some α0 ∈ (0, 1), uniformly at the relevant centers. Here µ can be a nonnegative measure; in the
application its density is individually bounded. Then Z has a uniform local Hölder modulus. The
same conclusion holds up to a constant Dirichlet face if the equation can be reflected with bounded
coefficients, bounded divergence flux, and the same source growth.

Proof. Choose a fixed large L and set Q± = e±LZ . The distributional chain rule gives

                  − div(A DQ± ) = div(±Le±LZ B)
                                     + e±LZ ± LF − L2 A DZ · DZ − L2 B · DZ .
                                               (                                  )


If the lower ellipticity bound is λ0 , choose L > 2a0 /λ0 . Young’s inequality absorbs the bounded-flux
cross term into the remaining negative gradient square. The boundedness of Z gives

                      − div(A DQ± ) ≤ div B± + C(dx + µ),          ∥B± ∥∞ ≤ C.                   (5.14)

The matrix on the left is the same for both signs.
    On Br , let w± be the zero-Dirichlet potential of the right side of (5.14). Scalar Dirichlet Green
estimates for bounded measurable uniformly elliptic coefficients give

                                        ∥w± ∥∞ ≤ C(r + rα0 ).                                    (5.15)

Here is the dependence on the source hypotheses. The bound G(x, y) ≤ C|x−y|    2−n and dyadic annuli
        α
give Cr for the measure source. The exterior-pole gradient energy bound {|x−y|>ρ} |Dy G(x, y)|2 ≤
                                                                            ∫
          0


Cρ2−n gives L1 gradient mass Cρ on an annulus of scale ρ. Summing gives Cr for bounded divergence
data. These scalar Green bounds hold in every n ≥ 3; see [15, Corollary 4.1 and Theorem 3.6]. One
can first use the individually bounded densities here and then pass to their uniform growth bounds.
    Put ϵr = C(r + rα0 ), and define

                                   U± = sup Q± + ϵr − Q± + w± .
                                          Br

They are nonnegative supersolutions. Let M = supBr Z and m = inf Br Z. On at least half of an
intermediate ball either Z ≤ (M + m)/2 or Z ≥ (M + m)/2. In the first case the uncorrected plus
deficit is bounded below by a fixed multiple of M − m; in the second case the minus deficit is. The
multiplicative constant is positive because Z is bounded. Weak Harnack for the corresponding U±
therefore lowers the supremum, or raises the infimum, on Br/4 , up to Cϵr . Thus

                             oscBr/4 Z ≤ (1 − c0 ) oscBr Z + C(r + rα0 ).

Iteration gives a Hölder modulus. The same proof applies after the stated reflection.


                                                   35
Lemma 5.3 (Estimates for smooth fixed points above the floor). Fix N and a sufficiently distant
truncation of MN . Every smooth solution of the system (4.17), or of its specified homotopy, satisfying
t > −ε, has local smooth bounds depending on fixed N and the background geometry. The bounds
hold up to the outer zero-Dirichlet face and are uniform as that face tends to infinity, on fixed
compact sets and uniformly sized end patches.

Proof. Lemma 4.6 and Proposition 4.7 give

                      −ε < t ≤ Z ≤ PN ,         ℓ ≤ l ≤ e,      |f | + |Df | ≤ ePN .

At fixed N , the eigenvalue χ = kd/(k + pv) is consequently bounded away from zero. The normalized
trace equation is                               √
                                         2        D(            )
                                  Aχ : ∇ f =         ηf − trAχ K .                           (5.16)
                                                  l
Its right side is bounded. Lemma 5.1 therefore bounds Df in C α , including at the outer face, and
gives (5.2) for f .
    Write the other equation in the form of Lemma 5.2, with

                                  A = kuAχ ,        B = uAχ K(w, ·).

The sign of the scalar source is immaterial for its absolute bound. The implicit relation for t gives

                                   |Dt| ≤ CN (1 + |DZ| + |D2 f |).

The explicit quadratic formula for T then bounds the absolute scalar source by

                                    CN (1 + |DZ|2 ) + CN |D2 f |2 .

All lower-order fluxes are bounded. The Hessian estimate supplies
                                      ∫
                                            |D2 f |2 ≤ CN rn−2+2α ;
                                       Br

we may decrease the exponent when applying Lemma 5.2. This proves a uniform Hölder modulus
for Z.
    At the outer face extend Z oddly and the divergence matrix by A− = RA+ R. Extend the
total flux by −R, and the scalar source oddly. The normal flux is continuous, so its distributional
divergence has no plane mass. Boundedness and the source-growth estimates are preserved. This
justifies the use of the boundary case of Lemma 5.2.
    The coefficients and right side of (5.16) are now Hölder, since they are smooth functions of
Z, Df on the bounded parameter range. Interior and Dirichlet Schauder estimates give f through
second derivatives. Expanding the divergence equation for Z, its principal matrix is Hölder and
its remaining source is bounded by CN (1 + |DZ|2 ). For finite s > n, use the local interpolation
inequality
                       ∥DZ∥2L2s (Q) ≤ C oscQ′ Z ∥D2 Z∥Ls (Q′ ) + CQ,Q′ (oscQ′ Z)2            (5.17)
on concentric ball or half-ball patches Q ⋐ Q′ . It follows by scaled derivative interpolation after
subtraction of a constant. The already proved Hölder estimate makes its Hessian coefficient uniformly
small on sufficiently small fixed patches. Local nondivergence W 2,s estimates therefore absorb the
quadratic term. The leading estimate constants stay bounded as patches shrink, since the principal
matrices have a uniform Hölder modulus. Lower-order constants may depend on the selected scale.

                                                    36
Use bounded overlap of patch enlargements and the supremum of their local Hessian norms to
carry out the absorption; these norms are individually finite on every truncation. This proves W 2,s
bounds for Z.
    Schauder estimates and differentiation now bootstrap both functions. At each differentiation
the f equation is solved first with the known regularity of Z, and then the expanded Z equation
gains the next derivative. These are the usual local and Dirichlet elliptic estimates [13]. All charts,
including uniformly sized end charts and outer boundary charts, have controlled geometry. Hence
the constants on fixed compact sets do not depend on the truncation radius.

5.3    Solving the trace equation and the coupled system
Lemma 5.4 (The trace equation for an arbitrary trial scalar). On a fixed smooth truncation, fix
Z ∈ C 1,α with zero outer trace. The trace equation F = ηf , with zero outer Dirichlet value for f ,
has a unique solution in C 3,α . The solution depends continuously on the trial Z and on the tensor
homotopy. On bounded sets of trials these solutions have bounded C 3,α norms. No floor condition
on the trial is needed.
Proof. For any prescribed Z, Df , the defining relation for t is strictly increasing in t, so the
coefficients are smooth functions of these variables. Continue the normalized equation (5.16) from
∆f = f , by straight interpolation of the two operators. Its height coefficient is strictly negative
when all terms are placed on the left. At a positive maximum the gradient vanishes, Aχ = I, and
the maximum test gives f ≤ ∥ tr K∥∞ /η; the analogous bound holds at a minimum. The same
height bound works throughout the interpolation.
   We supply the gradient bound, since ellipticity must not be assumed before it. Put σ = |Df |.
For bounded trial Z, as σ → ∞, the implicit relation has t < 0 and hence l = eN t , so
                                    log σ
                              t=−         + O(1),          d ≍ σ −2k/(k+N ) .
                                    k+N
For N > k this gives                               √
                                       cN            D
                                χ≥           ,         ≤ CN (1 + σ).                           (5.18)
                                     1+σ            l
The constants are uniform on a bounded set of trials. Compact σ ranges are included by positivity
and continuity. The normalized right side, using the height bound, is at most CN (1 + σ) in absolute
value. Straight operator interpolation has the same properties: its axial eigenvalue is at least χ.
    Choose a short distance interval [0, s0 ] and a modulus

                              ψ(0) = 0,      ψ ′′ = −K0 (ψ ′ )2 ,     ψ ′ > 0.

Choose K0 large first, then s0 sufficiently small for the distance geometry and so that ψ ′ ≥ 1 can
hold throughout. Finally choose the initial slope sufficiently large that ψ(s0 ) exceeds twice the
height bound. This is possible from

                                    ψ(s) = K0−1 log(1 + K0 ψ ′ (0)s).

On a boundary collar, ±ψ(dist(·, ∂)) are upper and lower barriers. The negative axial term χψ ′′ , by
(5.18), has size at least a fixed multiple of K0 ψ ′ , and dominates the linear-growth right side and the
bounded second derivatives of the distance.
    For the interior estimate consider a positive maximum of

                                      f (x) − f (y) − ψ(dist(x, y))

                                                    37
with distance less than s0 , using the smooth ambient metric extension across the outer boundary.
Contacts at a boundary endpoint are excluded by the preceding barriers; contacts at distance s0
are excluded by the height bound. At an interior contact the two gradients are parallel along the
short minimizing geodesic and have length σ = ψ ′ . Parallel transverse endpoint variations bound
the difference of the transverse Hessian traces by C dist(x, y)σ. The separate axial variations give

                                ∇2 fx (e, e) ≤ ψ ′′ ,        −∇2 fy (e, e) ≤ ψ ′′ .

Thus the difference of the two operators is at most

                                Cs0 σ + (χx + χy )ψ ′′ ≤ Cs0 σ − cN K0 σ.

For large K0 this contradicts the absolute linear-growth bound for the difference of their right sides.
The same argument with x, y exchanged proves the Lipschitz bound. It is valid throughout the
operator interpolation.
    The gradient bound gives uniform ellipticity. Apply Lemma 5.1, then Schauder to obtain C 2,β
for some β > 0. These bounds make Df Lipschitz, so the original C 1,α trial and the smooth
coefficient functions give C α coefficients; Schauder then yields C 2,α and, by one differentiation, C 3,α .
The Dirichlet linearization has a strictly negative zero-order term. The maximum principle and
the usual elliptic Fredholm alternative therefore make it invertible. The same maximum principle,
applied to the linear equation for the difference, gives uniqueness. Continuation is open by this
invertibility and closed by the uniform estimates. The implicit-function theorem and uniqueness
also give continuous dependence in the stated spaces.

Proposition 5.5 (A filled solution). For fixed prepared data and ε > 0, all sufficiently large N
admit a smooth solution of (4.17) on the entire one-ended filled manifold MN , with

                   t > −ε,       t ≤ Z ≤ PN ,           ℓ ≤ l ≤ e,         |f | + |∇f | ≤ ePN .

The functions f and all their derivatives decay exponentially on the end, with constants depending
on fixed N . Moreover

                         t, Z = Oj (r2−n )     for every j,          ∆g t = O(r−n−β ).               (5.19)

The uniform height bound and the radial height estimate of Lemma 4.4 hold on the limiting solution.

Proof. First truncate at a coordinate sphere of radius R, with R ≥ Rmin (N ) as in the floor estimate.
For a trial Z ∈ C 1,α with zero outer trace solve the trace equation by Lemma 5.4. Freeze this pair
in the divergence equation, except for the gradient of the new Z in its principal flux. The resulting
linear Dirichlet solve defines a map T into C 2,α . Indeed its principal coefficients are C 1,α , its drift
flux is C 1,α , and its scalar source is C α . Thus T is a continuous compact map on C 1,α . Fixed points
bootstrap to smoothness.
    Use the homotopy described with (4.17), in reverse order: first take K = 0 and scale the full
scalar source from zero to one, then scale K from zero to its specified value with the full source.
When K = 0, uniqueness of the trace equation gives f = 0 for every trial. At zero scalar scaling the
map is identically zero.
    For a homotopy parameter λ, write fλ [Z] for the trace solve. Apply degree on the relatively
open moving domain

                        {(λ, Z) : min t[Z, Dfλ [Z]] > −ε,           ∥Z∥C 1,α < MN,R } .


                                                        38
The dependence is continuous by Lemma 5.4. Choose MN,R larger than the fixed-point bound
supplied by Lemma 5.3. There is no norm-boundary fixed point. There is no floor-boundary
fixed point by the floor exclusion, including its outer-sphere estimate and final K = f = 0 stage.
The a priori estimates also hold with a non-strict floor inequality when taking a closure, before
that exclusion is applied. The map has relatively compact image on the bounded trial closure.
Leray–Schauder degree for a bounded relatively open subset of the product with the parameter
interval therefore applies [17, Sections 12–13]. Its degree at the initial zero map is one, by excision
around Z = 0; it stays one throughout the homotopy. This gives a smooth solution above the floor
on each sufficiently large truncation.
    Lemma 5.3 gives smooth local compactness uniform in R. To retain the boundary condition
at infinity, we establish uniform end estimates before exhausting. On the end K = 0, and (5.16)
becomes                                         √            √
                                        2     η D          η D
                                 Aχ : ∇ f =         f,            ≥ η/e.
                                                 l            l
For fixed N , sufficiently small κN > 0, and a sufficiently large fixed inner radius, the functions
CN e−κN (r−r0 ) are barriers for both signs of f . The positive zeroth-order coefficient dominates their
radial second derivatives; enlarge r0 to control the radial Hessian error. They dominate at the inner
sphere and at the outer zero boundary. Consequently f decays exponentially, uniformly in R. Local
and outer-face estimates give the same decay for every derivative. The implicit relation then gives
exponentially small t − Z, with all derivatives.
    Up to exponentially decaying errors, the end equation is

 k∆g Z + k (k − 1) + p(Z) − δ0 sp(Z) |dZ|2g = ρ − ϑ(N (Z + ε))CN ρ0 ,
           [                          ]
                                                                             sp = p + (k − 1)/2. (5.20)

Set aN (z) = (k − 1) + p(z) − δ0 sp(z) , and choose ΦN (0) = 0, Φ′N (0) = 1, with Φ′′N /Φ′N = aN . On the
bounded range of Z, Φ′N has positive lower and upper bounds depending on fixed N . Thus (5.20)
implies
                               ∆g ΦN (Z) = ON (r−n−β ) + ON (e−κN r ).
Comparison with multiples of r2−n (1 − r−β/2 ), on each truncation, gives ΦN (Z) = ON (r2−n ), hence
Z = ON (r2−n ). These barriers have the required signed Laplacian for large r; their r−n−β/2 term
dominates both the source and the asymptotically flat metric errors.
    Scaled Poisson W 2,s estimates first give weighted first-derivative Hölder bounds; the source is
a smooth function of Z, the radius weights and exponentially small terms. Scaled Schauder and
differentiation then give Z = Oj (r2−n ) for every j. The same follows for t. Its Laplacian has the
improved bound in (5.19), since |dZ|2 = O(r2−2n ) and β < 1. Exhaustion now supplies the asserted
solution with zero limit at infinity. If the limit touched −ε, that point would be an interior minimum,
and the identical floor calculation of Lemma 4.6 would exclude it. All height bounds pass to the
limit.

5.4   Mass, height separation, and the charged region

                           F ≥ −PN (ℓ + ℓ2 /η) = −PN (ℓ + ℓ1/2 ) = −oN (1).                       (5.22)

It has all-order metric decay Oj (r2−n ), a finite ADM energy, and scalar curvature O(r−n−β ).

Proof. All terms in uΞ are integrable. On the end this follows from the exponential decay of f ,
T = O(r2−2n ), and the integrable radius weight ρ0 = r−n−β ; on the filled compact part it is ordinary
smooth integrability. The divergence theorem on the manifold without an inner boundary proves
the flux limit in (5.21). At infinity V = k∇t + o(r1−n ) in the flux sense, since u = 1 + ON (r2−n )
and all graph terms decay exponentially. The metric perturbation 2tδij contributes −2k ∂i t to the
Euclidean ADM integrand. The normalization 1/(2kω) therefore gives the second identity in (5.21);
all mixed error fluxes vanish.
    Only the penalty band can contribute negatively to the integral. There m0 ̸= 0 implies
−ε < t < −ε + 1/N , and hence ℓ ≤ l ≤ eℓ for sufficiently large N . Writing σ = |∇f |g , the definitions

                         u ≤ C(ℓ + ℓ2 σ),    uv ≤ Cℓ2 σ,       uaw σ ≥ cℓ2 σ 2 .                  (5.24)

                                                  ℓ2

The integrals of ρ0 , ρ20 , ρ21 grow at most polynomially in N : the core volume is O(1 + N ), and on the

    A constant metric dilation by e2ε , followed by the corresponding coordinate dilation, multiplies
ADM energy by e(n−2)ε , proving (5.23). All-order metric decay follows from Proposition 5.5 and
the prepared end. The conformal scalar law, the prepared scalar tail, and ∆g t = O(r−n−β ) give the
stated scalar decay.

Proposition 5.7 (Separation of the original boundary by height). There are b2 > 0 and N2 ,
depending on the fixed prepared data and ε, such that the solutions of Proposition 5.5 satisfy, for
N ≥ N2 ,

      h < −b2    on every plus component of S,          h > b2   on every minus component of S.

The constants are independent of N . For every fixed b > 0, the portion of the original exterior on
which |h| ≥ b is contained in a fixed compact set, independent of N .

Proof. The last assertion follows immediately from the uniform height tail in Lemma 4.4. We prove
the first assertion by a trace supersolution on each long product and its prescribed collars. Only the
trace equation is used in this comparison, so no sign condition in the distant cap is needed.
    Treat a plus component. Choose a small fixed δ > 0 below the strict negative leaf-expansion
margin on the prescribed collars. On the middle of its long product use height −δ. There K = −L0 g,
with L0 the fixed large number used when constructing the filling. Towards the original boundary
let the slope increase from zero to a small positive fixed value a. It can be chosen as a smoothly



                                                   40
truncated exponential: start with size of order η, increase exponentially, and stop smoothly at a,
with
                                    |(slope)′ | ≤ C(slope + η).
Its length is O(1 + | log η|) = O(1 + N ), and its total height increase is as small as desired by
choosing a small. The initial smoothing from slope zero has the same displayed bound.
    For a height test H, the Hessian multiplier in the trace equation is
                                                       1
                                            √                     .
                                                (η/l)2 + |dH|2

Thus the axial Hessian contribution along this ramp is uniformly bounded, since l ≤ e and
                                             slope + η
                                        √                       ≤ C.
                                           (η/l)2 + slope2

The transverse contribution of the product metric is zero. The trace of −L0 g is at most −kL0 .
Taking L0 large compared with the fixed ramp bound makes the ramp a strict supersolution while
its height is at least −δ. This choice is part of the fixed filling geometry, before N .
    Continue at small positive slope through the fixed transitions to S, keeping the height there
below −δ/2. Then, on a fixed strict trapped collar on the original side, increase the slope smoothly
until the height exceeds the uniform coarse height bound. All these slopes are bounded below by
a > 0; the slopes and their derivatives on this fixed portion are fixed finite numbers. At a contact
with the solution, η/l ≤ ℓ1/2 , and the axial eigenvalue satisfies

                                                       (η/l)2
                                       χ≤d=                       .
                                                   (η/l)2 + |dH|2

Consequently the trace operator on the test converges uniformly, on that fixed portion, to the
expansion of its coordinate leaf, Hleaf + trleaf K. It is strictly negative by construction. The error
from the axial second derivative tends to zero as N → ∞. With the original choice of δ, this trace
is below the test height, including while the height increases to dominate the coarse bound.
    On the side of the long product facing the cap, mirror the ramp and continue farther into
product geometry at fixed slope, with height increasing until it also dominates the coarse bound.
This adds only a fixed amount of length after the O(N ) ramp. It closes the comparison region
without assuming anything about the values of h in the cap, even when opposite-sign products join
there.
    The test therefore dominates h on both ends of the comparison region and is a strict supersolution
within it. At a positive interior maximum of h − H, the two gradients agree. The implicit t, l, and
Aχ consequently agree when evaluating the test with the actual Z, while the Hessian of h − H is
nonpositive. The trace equation would give h ≤ F (H) < H, a contradiction. Thus h ≤ H < −δ/2
on S. For a minus component apply the same argument to −h, −K: D, t, l, χ are unchanged
under the reversal, and the trace equation reverses sign. Taking the minimum over finitely many
components gives b2 . These barriers concern the final system; the homotopy stages need not satisfy
this comparison.

6     Conversion of electric flux into an energy decrease
This section completes the numerical argument. The filled scalar construction provides a metric
which dominates the original metric and has the required charged scalar-curvature inequality on a
region separated from the original boundary. A second scalar equation converts the signed total
electric flux into a controlled decrease of ADM energy. A lower bound for its conformal factor retains
a definite fraction of every full-cut area. We then construct a smooth future-trapped boundary and
apply only the neutral numerical input, Theorem 2.4.

                                                             42
6.1    Data supplied by the filled construction
Throughout this section, n ≥ 4, k = n − 1, ω = Area(S k ), and c2n = k(k − 1)/2. Initially the exterior
data have been prepared as in Proposition 3.8 and Lemma 3.6. We write g for their metric, E∗ for
their ADM energy, and

                             a∗ = inf Areag (Γ),              X∗ = (a∗ /ω)(k−1)/k .                        (6.1)
                                      Γ

The infimum is over the entire full cuts of this prepared exterior. In particular, a∗ > 0 by Lemma 3.1.
The prepared tensor K has compact support, the metric has differentiated decay of order 2 − n
to every fixed order, and the charged dominant-energy and prescribed componentwise trapping
inequalities are strict. The optional compact magnetic form is allowed in this paragraph; it will be
specified again in Corollary 6.7.
    Fix ε > 0. For every sufficiently large approximation integer N , let MN be the filled one-ended
manifold of Proposition 5.5. We also denote its background metric by g. It agrees with the prepared
metric on the original exterior. Put

      ℓ = e−N ε ,   η = ℓ3/2 ,       h = ηf,        z = t + ε,         ḡ = g + l2 df 2 ,   gb = e2z ḡ.   (6.2)

The scalar-construction variables satisfy

                             z ≥ 0,         ℓ ≤ l ≤ e,         |dh|ḡ ≤ η/l ≤ ℓ1/2 .                       (6.3)

For fixed prepared data and ε, the bounds for t, Z, and ∇f imply that the metric coefficients and
inverse coefficients needed below have bounds of the form exp(PN ), where PN denotes a polynomial
in 1 + N whose coefficients may change from occurrence to occurrence. No uniform bound as
N → ∞ is claimed for the second scalar solution.
    We use the following further conclusions of Propositions 5.6, 5.7, and 5.8. There are constants
b2 > b3 > 0, independent of N , such that

                                 |h| > b2   on S,        h −→ 0       at infinity,                         (6.4)

and the set {|h| ≥ b3 /4} on the original exterior is contained in a fixed compact set. We take
b3 < b2 /2. On
                          UN = {x in the original exterior : |h(x)| ≤ b3 },                (6.5)
the retained curvature estimate is the charged one, with an additional positive margin on b3 /4 ≤
|h| ≤ b3 . More precisely, the scalar identity supplies there a constant c∗ > 0, independent of N , and
the nonnegative term (1 − δ0 )T , with δ0 < 1/2. The energy estimate, in coordinates normalized for
gb at infinity, is

                    E(gb ) ≤ e(k−1)ε (E∗ + oN (1)),             oN (1) −→ 0    (N −→ ∞).                   (6.6)

The fixed-N metric gb has all-order differentiated asymptotic decay of order 2 − n after the constant
coordinate dilation, and Rgb = O(r−n−β ) for a β > 0.
     Let α = iE volg on the original exterior. Multiply it by sgn Q when Q ̸= 0, and use either sign
when Q = 0. Extend this signed form smoothly into the filling, cutting it off on fixed collars on the
inside of S; call the extension α̃. It is closed on the original exterior and need not be closed in the
filling. Define
                                    iEb volgb = α̃,    iEext volg = α̃.                            (6.7)


                                                         43
Thus the flux of Eb across a large sphere is ω|Q|. Its divergence vanishes on the original exterior.
The vector Eext is uniformly bounded on the filling and is O(r−k ) on the end. Its support in the
filling can be kept in the fixed collars; even an extension over the whole core would have only
polynomially growing Lp norms, because that core has volume O(1 + N ).
     Matrix formulas below use a g-orthonormal frame, or equivalently the g-identification of vectors
and covectors. For clarity, the form norm underlying the charged estimate is

                          |α|ḡ2 = (1 − |w|2g )|E|2g + g(E, w)2 = |E|2g − |w ∧ E|2g .                        (6.8)
                                                  √
Indeed ḡ −1 = Ad = g −1 − w ⊗ w and volḡ = D volg . Completing the square in the magnetic
two-form, when it is present, gives Ig (w) ≥ c2n |α|ḡ2 . Since z ≥ 0,

                                     e2z |Eb |2gb = e−2(k−1)z |α|ḡ2 ≤ |α|ḡ2 .                              (6.9)

Consequently the scalar-construction estimates imply Rgb /2 ≥ c2n |Eb |2gb throughout UN .

Lemma 6.1 (A retained gradient term). The quadratic expression T in the filled scalar construction
satisfies
                                                k2 − 1

Proof. We give the estimates on expanding smooth coordinate-sphere truncations of MN , with zero

                                                           45
A background Sobolev inequality. The background filled metric has uniformly controlled local
geometry. Its core is covered by at most C(1 + N ) coordinate patches of fixed size and bounded
overlap; the product necks account for the number of patches. The overlap graph is connected to
a fixed annulus in the unchanged asymptotic end by chains of length at most C(1 + N ). For a
compactly supported smooth function v, radial integration from infinity and Cauchy–Schwarz give,
on this fixed annulus A,                        ∫
                                   ∥v∥2L2 (A,g) ≤ C            |dv|2g volg .                    (6.25)
                                                         end

For example, the radial weight in this estimate is finite because R∞ r1−n dr < ∞. Local Poincaré
                                                                               ∫

inequalities control the oscillation about each patch mean. On overlapping patches the difference of
the two means is controlled by the gradient energy on their union. Summing along the chains to A,
then summing over the patches, therefore gives
                                                                ∫
                             ∥v∥2L2 (core,g) ≤ C(1 + N )C             |dv|2g volg .             (6.26)
                                                                 MN

Combine this with the local Sobolev inequalities on the patches and the ordinary Euclidean Sobolev
inequality on the end, using a cutoff across A. With p∗ = 2n/(n − 2), this proves
                                           ∫
                        ∥v∥2Lp∗ (g) ≤ SN        |dv|2g volg ,         SN ≤ exp(PN ).            (6.27)
                                           MN

The polynomial bound implicit above would suffice. The weaker displayed bound is convenient for
combining constants. Functions with zero trace on a truncation can be extended by zero, so the
same constant works for all outer radii. The local Sobolev and Poincaré inequalities used here are
the usual uniformly elliptic coordinate versions; see [13, Chapters 7–8].

Existence on a truncation and exhaustion. Fix N and an outer radius. For a continuous
trial function Y of zero boundary value, freeze h(Y )Eext and solve the linear Dirichlet equation
(6.30). On this fixed smooth domain its coefficients are smooth and uniformly elliptic. Linear
divergence-form W 1,p estimates with any fixed p > n show that this solution operator is continuous
and compact into continuous functions of zero boundary value. Fixed points of its homotopy with
the zero map satisfy (6.35). Leray–Schauder degree on a larger ball therefore gives a fixed point,
since the degree at the zero map is one; see [17, Sections 12–17].
    For such a solution the bounded divergence data first give local Hölder estimates. Then
h(Y )Eext is Hölder continuous, divergence Schauder estimates give C 1,α regularity, and successive
differentiation gives smoothness. These estimates, with the supremum bound, are uniform on each
fixed compact subset as the outer radius tends to infinity. The standard interior and Dirichlet
estimates used at this step apply to smooth uniformly elliptic scalar operators on fixed charts; see
[13, Chapters 6 and 8]. A diagonal subsequence thus gives a smooth solution on MN , preserving
the energy and supremum bounds.

The floor and inversion. On a truncation, test (6.30) by (Y0 − Y )+ . Its support is a region on
which h(Y ) = 0. Ellipticity gives zero gradient for this test function, and its outer boundary trace is
zero because Y0 < 0. Hence Y ≥ Y0 . This passes to the limit. Lemma 6.2 now defines y = B0−1 (Y )
everywhere. The inverse is smooth at Y0 as well, since B0′ (y0 ) > 0. Inequality (6.21) and (6.35) give
y ≤ PN , while the floor is y ≥ y0 .

The end and the total flux.           On the end divgb Eb = 0, so the equation for Y is

                                             k∆gb Y = h′ (Y )Eb (Y ).                            (6.36)


                                                            47
The drift coefficient is O(r−k ), with fixed-N bounds. Choose 0 < β ′ < min(1, n−2). After increasing
                                                           ′
the fixed inner radius, positive multiples of r2−n (1 − r−β ) are supersolutions of the absolute drift
                                                                                  ′
comparison problem: their leading negative Laplacian term has order r−n−β , and dominates the
metric-error and first-order terms. Comparison with both signs, using the uniform bound on the
fixed inner sphere and zero data at the truncation sphere, gives Y = O(r2−n ) uniformly during
exhaustion. Scaled elliptic estimates for (6.36) then give the first two differentiated orders, and the
same is true for y.
    Since supp H0′ ⋐ (y0 , 0) and y → 0, one has H0 (y) = H0 (0) identically sufficiently far out. There
Y is harmonic for gb . The all-order metric estimates for gb and scaled harmonic estimates yield
Y, y = Oj (r2−n ) for every fixed j. Integrating (6.22) on the whole filled truncation now gives, for
every sufficiently large radius,
                          ∫                              ∫
                      k            ∂νb Y dAgb = H0 (0)        gb (Eb , νb ) dAgb = H0 (0)ω|Q|.    (6.37)

this integral has an error O(R2−n ). This proves (6.24).

Proposition 6.6 (Prepared numerical bound). For the prepared data, for every 0 < s < 1,

                                                                  1 − s2
                                              E∗ ≥ s|Q| +                X∗ .                                 (6.56)
                                                                    2
The conclusion also holds for prepared data carrying a smooth compactly supported closed magnetic
two-form, with the charged energy convention used in the filled scalar construction.


                                                             50
Proof. Keep s, a smooth profile, and ε fixed. Take N sufficiently large for all preceding constructions.
Sard’s theorem gives a regular value ch ∈ (b3 /2, b3 ) of |h|. Let DN be the closure of the component
of {|h| < ch } containing the entire far end. By (6.4), this component cannot cross S into the filling.
Its entire boundary is a compact smooth embedded two-sided hypersurface in the original interior.
It is nonempty: a path in the original connected exterior from the far end to S must cross the
chosen level. All boundary components are retained.
    The mean curvature of this fixed compact boundary in γ is finite. We may now choose the
plateau M in Lemma 6.5 so large that, with normal toward the end,

                   H∂DN + tr∂DN Knew = H∂DN − ka < 0           on every component.                (6.57)

This late choice does not require repeating either scalar solve or increasing N , by (6.55). Restricted
to DN , the new tensor has compact support, since h → 0 and H vanishes near zero.
    We check the neutral input’s hypotheses explicitly. The exterior DN is connected and oriented,
smooth up to its nonempty compact boundary, and has exactly the original coordinate end with
compact complement. It is complete with boundary included: it is closed in the original complete
exterior, and (6.39) bounds its metric below by a positive constant times g. The metric is smooth
and has all-order differentiated decay of order 2 − n in normalized end coordinates. Thus the metric
and tensor satisfy the stronger falloffs in Theorem 2.4, with any exponent strictly between (n − 2)/2
and n − 2. The neutral DEC follows from Lemma 6.5; its matter densities are integrable because
the tensor is compactly supported and the scalar curvature is integrable at infinity. The ADM
momentum is zero. The ADM energy exists and is given by (6.40). Finally the new boundary is
strictly future outer trapped by (6.57).
    Every full cut of DN , regarded as a subset of the original exterior, is also a full cut there. This
includes any portions coincident with ∂DN , and counts every component. Therefore its full-cut
infimum aN satisfies
                                            aN ≥ eky0 a∗ > 0.                                      (6.58)
Indeed the metric comparison acts on all k tangent directions, and the class of available cuts has
only been restricted. No minimizing boundary is assumed to be smooth or to exist.
   It remains to verify the timelike energy hypothesis of the neutral input for these auxiliary data.
Write eN = E(γ). If eN > 0, their zero momentum makes that hypothesis immediate. If eN ≤ 0,
apply the conformal end increase of Lemma 3.3, taking its positive function ϕ constant on a core
containing the boundary and the new tensor’s support. For U = 1 + λϕ, transform the data by

                              γλ = U 4/(n−2) γ,      Kλ = U 2/(n−2) Knew .                        (6.59)

The neutral DEC and trapping signs persist, the stronger asymptotic derivatives persist, momentum
remains zero, and full-cut areas do not decrease. Its energy is eN + 2λ. For every arbitrarily small
e > 0 choose λ = (e − eN )/2 ≥ 0. These data satisfy all hypotheses of Theorem 2.4, which would
give
                                   1                 1
                               e ≥ (aN /ω)(k−1)/k ≥ e(k−1)y0 X∗ > 0.                           (6.60)
                                   2                 2
Letting e ↓ 0 is a contradiction. Thus eN > 0, and the same neutral input applied directly gives

                                              H0 (0)      1              1 − s2
                    e(k−1)ε (E∗ + oN (1)) −          |Q| ≥ e(k−1)y0 X∗ =        X∗ .              (6.61)
                                               k          2                2
   The parameter order is as follows. For the fixed prepared data, fix s ∈ (0, 1), a profile tolerance,
and ε > 0 first. Complete the two scalar solves and the regular cut for each sufficiently large N ; the

                                                    51
tensor plateau is chosen last for that N . Now let N → ∞ in (6.61), then let ε ↓ 0, and then let the
profile tolerance decrease to zero. Equations (6.15) and (6.12) give (6.56). Only fixed-parameter
estimates were required in the second scalar solve. The electromagnetic input used in this proof was
precisely (6.8) and its lower bound by Ig (w), which also holds for the indicated compact magnetic
form.

6.5   Return to the original data and optimize
Corollary 6.7 (Strict compact-magnetic numerical extension). Let the original exterior satisfy
the numerical geometric and asymptotic hypotheses, and let Dm be an arbitrary smooth compactly
supported closed two-form. With α = iE volg closed, use the electromagnetic convention

            Ig (v) = c2n |E|2g + |Dm |2g + 2Dm (v, E) ,
                       (                             )
                                                           µm + Jm (v) = µ + J(v) − Ig (v).       (6.62)

Assume also that µm , |Jm |g are integrable and that the ADM and charge flux limits exist with the
stated normalizations. Assume the ADM vector is future timelike, the fixed componentwise trapping
inequalities are strict, and for some c > 0 and 0 < β < min(1, q),

                                         µm − |Jm |g ≥ cr−n−β .                                   (6.63)

Then, with the original full-cut infimum a and the original normalized electric charge Q,
                                               1 − s2
                                 m ≥ s|Q| +           XA   (0 < s < 1).                           (6.64)
                                                 2
Equivalently, m ≥ |Q|, and when XA > |Q| one also has m ≥ (XA + Q2 /XA )/2. No magnetic
equality classification or non-timelike extension is asserted here.
Proof. Apply the rest preparation and strict preparation in Proposition 3.8 and Lemma 3.6, retaining
the two closed forms. The magnetic support stays in the compact original region. There the strictly
positive margin absorbs the arbitrarily small rescaling errors. The gluing and bending annuli can be
taken outside that support, so on those annuli and on the end the electric estimates are unchanged.
This yields prepared data with charge Q, energies E∗,j → m, and full-cut infima a∗,j ≥ (1 − oj (1))a.
For each fixed s, apply Proposition 6.6 and then pass to this limit. The right side is increasing in
the area variable, so the lower comparison of infima is sufficient. This proves (6.64). The elementary
optimization is given in Proposition 3.10. The argument does not require the component electric
fluxes to have one sign.

Completion of Theorem 2.5. Proposition 6.6 establishes (3.20) for every prepared data set with
positive energy and zero momentum, and every fixed s ∈ (0, 1). The Reduction of the Numerical
Theorem (Proposition 3.10) therefore proves Theorem 2.5, including positivity of the full enclosing
area, strict future timelikeness of the original ADM vector, and the stated branch distinction. The
endpoint limits in s are taken after the fixed-s inequality is established, so no profile estimate
uniform at s = 0 or s = 1 is required.


7     The original-data variational identity
We now assume the connected-horizon hypotheses of Definition 2.6 and equality in Theorem 2.5,
with m > |Q|. All objects in this section belong to the original exterior (Ω, g, K, E). In particular, no
equality statement about one of the auxiliary metrics is used. We first differentiate the full enclosing-
area infimum, then separate the active constraint inequalities from a direction that would improve

                                                    52
equality. The resulting multipliers are initially measures. Their regularity and the elimination of
their possible singular area sheets are separate steps. Decrease the asymptotic exponent, if necessary,
so that (n − 2)/2 < q < n − 2; this retains every original hypothesis.
    Write A = Areag (S), k = n − 1, and
                                                                                    (             )
               1/k         0  EADM           i   (PADM )i                  k−1      Q2
   rh = (A/ω)        ,    b =      ,        b =−          ,             c=     1 − 2(k−1)             > 0.   (7.1)
                               m                   m                        rh    r           h

The vector b is fixed when taking variations. Set B = b0 EADM + i bi (PADM )i . Thus m′ = B ′ at
                                                                             ∑

No magnetic equality theorem is assumed. Compactly supported closed variations of either form,
including tests supported up to S, do not change Q, by Stokes’ theorem on a truncation.

7.1   The full area envelope
Choose a compact smooth filling O behind S, with a smooth extension of the metric, and denote
the resulting boundary-free manifold by M. The filling is used only to define perimeter competitors;
no constraint inequality is required there. A filled competitor is a bounded finite-perimeter set
F ⊂ M containing O up to null sets. Its perimeter counts the part coinciding with ∂O = S.

Proposition 7.1 (Area envelope and regular patches). The least perimeter of filled competitors
equals the full smooth-cut infimum a. The family F∗ of minimizing filled sets, considered modulo
null sets, is compact in local L1 , and all its members are supported in a common compact set. Their
exterior end components have the full-boundary convention of Definition 2.3.
    Each minimizing frontier is smooth and minimal at its free regular points. Its possible singular
set has Hausdorff dimension at most n − 8. All contact points with S are regular of class C 1,γ for
some γ > 0, and their graph representations belong to W 2,p for every finite p. At an interior regular
point of one frontier, any other minimizing frontier through the point coincides with it locally. On a
smaller neighborhood the minimizing frontiers that meet that neighborhood are disjoint single graphs,
with uniform smooth interior estimates.
    For a differentiable metric path with g0′ = h, the right derivative of its enclosing-area infimum is
                                                                1
                                                                    ∫
                         a′+ (h) = min a′F (h),     a′F (h) =             trT ∂ ∗ F h dAg .                  (7.4)
                                   F ∈F∗                        2   ∂∗F

Here and below ∂ ∗ F is used for integration, while “frontier” means the support of its perimeter
measure.

                                                     53
Proof. Large coordinate spheres have positive mean curvature, uniformly for the finite-dimensional
metric paths considered below. On the region outside one such sphere, the outward unit normal to
their foliation has positive divergence. Applying the divergence theorem to the portion of a filled
competitor outside that sphere shows that clipping off that portion cannot increase perimeter, and
strictly decreases it if the discarded portion has positive volume. The argument holds for almost
every cutting radius by finite-perimeter slicing. Choose two fixed radii, both outside the compact
core. Clipping at the smaller radius gives a common compact truncation, strictly inside the larger
one, for minimization and for all sufficiently small parameters of each path. BV compactness and
lower semicontinuity now give a minimizer; see [1].
     A smooth full cut bounds a filled competitor with exactly the same area, including its contact
portions. Conversely, first push O and an arbitrary filled competitor slightly outward by a smooth
ambient diffeomorphism supported in a collar of S. The pushed competitor contains a neighborhood
of the original O, and its perimeter tends to the original perimeter. Strict approximation of finite-
perimeter sets by smooth domains can therefore be carried out while retaining that neighborhood.
It is supported inside a fixed compact truncation. Retain the exterior component containing the end,
filling any bounded exterior components of the smoothed set. This only removes boundary compo-
nents. Its entire smooth intrinsic boundary is a full cut. Passing through these two approximations
proves equality of the infima. The same removal observation shows that a minimizing frontier cannot
leave a removable bounded complementary open component: a nonempty such component has posi-
tive perimeter, whose removal would improve the minimum. The usual indecomposable-component
decomposition gives the same conclusion for finite-perimeter representatives [1].
     Here is the local estimate needed at the obstacle. For a local competitor G, replace it by G ∪ O.
Submodularity gives
                                P (G ∪ O) ≤ P (G) + P (O) − P (G ∩ O).                           (7.5)
Extend the smooth outward normal of O to a smooth vector field of norm at most one near its
boundary. Its divergence is bounded. The calibration inequality for this field gives P (O)−P (G∩O) ≤
C|O \ G| for changes in a sufficiently small coordinate neighborhood. Since F minimizes among sets
containing O, it follows that
                                   P (F ; B) ≤ P (G; B) + C|F △ G|                              (7.6)
whenever F △ G is compactly contained in the small ball B. This is an unconstrained perimeter
almost-minimizing estimate. In coordinates the Riemannian area integrand is the ellipsoidal
integrand associated with A = (det g)g −1 , which is smooth and uniformly elliptic. The error in (7.6)
is O(rn ) and therefore is bounded by Cγ rn−1+γ for any fixed 0 < γ < 1 on sufficiently small balls.
The almost-minimizer results in [24, Propositions 4.5–4.7 and Theorems 1.1, 9.5, 10.2–10.3] apply.
They give density and perimeter bounds, local compactness, regularity off a set of dimension at
most n − 8, and convergence as single C 1 graphs near free regular limit points. On free patches, the
uniform local C 1,γ/4 graph bounds and the smooth minimal-graph equation upgrade this to smooth
convergence on smaller patches by interior elliptic estimates. Tangent sets are perimeter-minimizing
cones. The minimal-boundary formulation and dimension reduction are also described in [25,
Chapter 7, Section 5].
    At a contact point a tangent filled cone contains the tangent half-space of O. The corresponding
minimizing hypercone is therefore supported on one side of its boundary plane, and is that plane. One
way to see the last assertion is to integrate the signed coordinate-height equation on its stationary
spherical link; the height has one sign and its spherical Laplacian is a negative constant times itself.
The singular set can be removed in this integration by its dimension and the perimeter bounds.
Equivalently one can use the half-space cone theorem in [25, Chapter 7, Section 4, Theorem 4.5].
The almost-minimizer regularity theorem now gives a C 1,β graph at contact for some β > 0; one may

                                                  54
take β = γ/4. Estimate (7.6), differentiated along smooth flows, bounds its weak mean curvature.
Difference quotients in the uniformly elliptic prescribed-mean-curvature graph equation give W 2,2
regularity. Written in nondivergence form, that equation has Hölder coefficients and bounded
right-hand side, so local W 2,p estimates give all finite p; see [13].
    If two minimizing frontiers meet at a free regular point of one of them, their union and intersection
also minimize by (7.5), with equality in the sum of the perimeters. They are ordered relative to
the given regular sheet. Their tangent cones at contact are one-sided relative to its tangent plane,
and hence are planes. Regularity and the strong comparison principle for the minimal graph
equation imply local coincidence. Strict compactness and regular convergence then give the asserted
uniform graph neighborhoods: otherwise a sequence of failing frontiers would converge strictly
to a minimizer through the point, where multiplicity-one regular convergence gives precisely the
missing neighborhood. The difference of two distinct ordered graphs solves a linear uniformly elliptic
equation. Positive-solution Harnack and interior derivative estimates imply, on smaller patches, that
its gradient is bounded by a uniform constant times its pointwise separation.
    Finally let Ft minimize for gt . Compactness, lower semicontinuity and comparison with a fixed
g0 minimizer show that every convergent subsequence at t = 0 converges strictly in perimeter to an
element of F∗ . Strict continuity for vector measures then gives convergence of the tangent-plane
area measures [1]. The area Taylor expansion has a uniform remainder on the resulting perimeter-
bounded family. Fixed minimizers give the upper bound in (7.4); the varying minimizers give the
lower bound. This also proves compactness of the minimizing family and continuity of F ↦ a′F (h)
in its strict topology.

7.2   Strict directions and positive-measure separation
An active ray is a pair (x, v) with |v|g ≤ 1 and µm (x) + Jm (x)(v) = 0. Under a metric variation h,
transport the unit ball by the metric square root, so v ′ = −h♯ v/2. Denote by C′ the variation of
2(µm + Jm (v)) under that transport. Our test space consists of finite linear combinations of:
  (i) arbitrary compact smooth variations (h, p, α′ , Dm
                                                       ′ ), including support up to S, with p = K ′

      and both form variations closed;
 (ii) a metric end prototype h = r2−n δ, cut off toward the core;
(iii) second-tensor end prototypes whose Euclidean trace reversal is

                            Πij = r−k ai nδj + aj nδi − (a · nδ )δij ,    nδ = x/r,
                                       (                            )
                                                                                                   (7.7)

      with arbitrary constant a ∈ Rn and a cutoff toward the core;
 (iv) the strict conformal direction (h, p) = (2ϕg, ϕK), with both forms fixed.
For the last direction choose 0 < β < min(1, q), put ρ0 = r−n−β with a positive smooth extension of
the radial weight, and take
                              √
                    −∆ϕ = B |dϕ|2 + r−2k + ρ0 ,            ∂ν ϕ = −1,       ϕ(∞) = 0,              (7.8)

where B ≥ |K| is smooth and has simple symbol bounds B = Oj (r−1−q ). The strictification
construction in Lemma 3.6 applies to this decaying drift: its radial barriers dominate the drift
because β < q. It gives ϕ > 0, ϕ = O2 (r2−n ) and finite gradient flux. In particular

                             −2k∆ϕ + 2kK(∇ϕ, v) + 4(k − 1)c2n ϕ|E|2 .                          (7.10)
    The prototypes have zero flat linearized constraints on the end. For example Πij = r−n (ai xj +
aj xi − (a · x)δij ) satisfies ∂j Πij = 0 and Πij nj = r−k ai . Its momentum derivative is ai /k; the scalar
prototype has energy derivative (n − 2)/2. The background errors in the linearized constraints are
O(r−n−q ). Thus the prototypes span the ADM flux derivatives and their normalized active-constraint
values tend to zero.

Proposition 7.2 (Multiplier identity). There are a probability measure π on F∗ , a nonnegative
locally finite measure Λ on the active rays, and a nonnegative finite measure ζ on S, such that
                                      ∫                        ∫            ∫
                                 ′
                           2kωB − c         a′F (h) dπ(F ) =        ′
