                                                                                    θ+ dζ            (7.11)
                                       F∗                                   S

for every compact test and every end prototype above. No surjectivity of a constraint map is required.

Proof. Consider the linear image of the test space with components
                                ( ′        ′
                                             , {ca′F (h) − 2kωB ′ }F ∈F∗ .
                                                                                )
                                 C /ρ0 , −θ+                                                         (7.12)

The constraint test space is the one-point compactification of the closed active-ray set, with one
artificial infinity point added even if that set is bounded. At infinity assign 2k times the coefficient
of the strict direction. The other two test spaces are S and the compact minimizing family. The
normalized functions are continuous by (7.9) and Proposition 7.1.
    This image does not meet the open cone of functions strictly positive on the disjoint union
of the three compact test spaces. Indeed such a direction has positive strict-direction coefficient
because of the infinity test. Apply that positive conformal direction exponentially to the data
after making the other variations additively. On compact subsets, strict first-order positivity on
the active rays implies feasibility for all sufficiently small positive parameters: rays outside a fixed
neighborhood of the active set have a positive zeroth-order gap. On the end the nonconformal linear
errors are O(r−n−q ) and are dominated by ρ0 . After retaining the nonnegative background through
the conformal formula, the quadratic errors are bounded by Ct2 ρ0 . The strict first-order term
therefore gives µm − |Jm | ≥ ct ρ0 , with ct > 0. The forms remain closed; a newly created magnetic
form has fixed compact support. Trapping is strict, the ADM vector remains future timelike, and
completeness, decay, integrability and existence and differentiability of the fluxes are preserved.
These are precisely the hypotheses of Corollary 6.7. The area infimum stays positive by uniform
metric comparison on the compact core and the controlled tail. Equation (7.4) and strict positivity
of the objective components would now violate the numerical inequality for small positive parameter.
    Hahn–Banach separation of a linear subspace from an open convex cone gives a nonzero positive
functional on the continuous functions on this compact disjoint union. The Riesz representation
theorem supplies the three nonnegative measures. Its objective measure has positive mass. Otherwise
testing the strict direction, uniformly positive on the two constraint test spaces, would force both
remaining measures to vanish as well. Normalize the objective measure to have mass one. On finite
active rays divide the constraint measure by ρ0 to obtain Λ. This measure is finite over compact
subsets, including the boundary. The possible infinity atom contributes nothing on compact tests or
prototypes. Rearranging the annihilation identity gives (7.11).




                                                     56
7.3   Normal-motion tests and the sheets selected by the multiplier
Let u and X be the scalar and vector moments of Λ. Initially these are measures, using the fixed
Riemannian volume-density convention; thus u also denotes the scalar distribution defined by its
moment measure. Positivity and the definition of the active set imply
                                     u ≥ |X|g ,       uµm + Jm (X) = 0                            (7.13)
as measure identities. In particular, electromagnetic or metric terms of the ray derivative require
moments of order at most one.
Lemma 7.3 (Tangential trace on free sheets). For π-almost every minimizing frontier, trT ∂F K = 0
on each of its free regular sheets. Every such frontier either equals S, or is wholly inside int Ω and
satisfies H = trT ∂F K = 0 on its regular part.
Proof. Fix a smooth lapse test s compactly supported in the open exterior, and choose a local
Gaussian Lorentzian metric inducing g, K at time zero. Its spatial metric and first normal jet
are fixed, while its second normal metric jets are free. Choose a two-form whose initial electric
and spatial components are cn E and zero. Its first normal derivatives can be chosen so that both
Maxwell equations hold on the initial slice. In general dimension the tangential Maxwell constraints
are dDm = 0 and dα = 0; the remaining equations prescribe the n(n − 1)/2 magnetic and n electric
normal evolution components. The induced flux-form variations under normal motion with velocity
sN are compactly supported and closed, as follows also from Cartan’s formula applied to these
initial Maxwell jets.
    Let S denote the electromagnetic stress tensor with the normalization in Definition 2.2. It is
divergence-free at the initial slice. For each δ > 0, prescribe there the spatial components of Einstein
minus S to be
                                              (δ)  (Jm )i (Jm )j
                                            Tij =                .                                 (7.14)
                                                     µm + δ
Spatial trace reversal of the second normal metric jets is invertible, so these are legitimate smooth jet
prescriptions. The normal and mixed components remain µm , Jm by Gauss–Codazzi. The resulting
variation has h = 2sK.
    We check that C′ → 0 uniformly on compact active test sets. The tensors in (7.14) converge
spatially in C 1 . On {µm > 0} this is ordinary smooth convergence; near a zero of µm , DEC gives
|Jm | ≤ µm and smoothness gives dµm = ∇Jm = 0 at that zero. The bounds
                             |T (δ) | ≤ µm ,       |∇T (δ) | ≤ 2|∇Jm | + |dµm |                   (7.15)
make the convergence uniform across the zero set. At a positive-density active ray, the limiting
spacetime tensor is γ ⊗ γ/µm , where γ(N ) = µm and γ|T Ω = Jm . It is causal and annihilates the
null vector N + v. Parallel-transport that null vector along spatial curves. Its contraction with
the causal covector is nonnegative and vanishes at the base point; differentiation at the minimum
therefore shows that all relevant spatial covariant derivatives, with last argument N + v, vanish.
Bianchi and the initial Maxwell equations then give the same limiting vanishing for the normal
derivative contracted with N, N + v. Changes of the first argument cost zero, and changes of the
second cost zero because it remains null and γ is proportional to its metric dual. At a matter zero,
both the tensor and its spatial derivatives vanish by (7.15), and the divergence equation again
suffices. These observations prove the asserted uniform convergence of C′ .
    Apply (7.11) to these tests and pass to the limit. They have no boundary or ADM variation, so
                                 ∫      ∫
                                               s trT ∂ ∗ F K dAg dπ(F ) = 0.                      (7.16)
                                   F∗   ∂∗F


                                                       57
At free regular points, Proposition 7.1 provides uniform noncrossing graph patches, and frontiers
through a common point have the same tangent plane. Consequently the tangential trace is a
single-valued continuous function on each such patch of the integrated surface measure. There is no
cancellation between different tangent planes in (7.16). A countable cover and Fubini imply the
first conclusion, initially almost everywhere and then everywhere on regular sheets by smoothness.
    At contact, the W 2,p graph and the smooth obstacle have the same second jets almost everywhere
on their coincidence set. The frontier therefore satisfies H+trtan K = 0 almost everywhere throughout
the contact graph, including its free portion. Uniform ellipticity first improves its regularity and
then bootstraps it to a smooth solution of the expansion equation. The strong comparison principle
gives coincidence with S near contact. The set of coincidence is open and closed along the connected
S, so the whole S is present. Its area is already the minimum A, leaving no area for another sheet.
If there is no contact, the compact frontier is separated from S, and the stated equations hold on
all its regular points.

7.4    The moment equations and their initial regularity
Proposition 7.4 (Regularity of the measure moments). In the open exterior the moments are
functions satisfying the first Killing initial data (KID) equation

                                           sym ∇X = −uK,                                           (7.17)

and
                                                    c
                                                        ∫
                         Hess u = L(u, X, ∇X) +              νF♭ ⊗ νF♭ dAF dπ(F ).                 (7.18)
                                                    2   F∗
The equations are distributional, and surface measures in the second equation are expressed relative
to ambient volume. The operator L has smooth linear coefficients. It is specifically the lower-order
operator from the metric adjoint in (7.11), with the flux form fixed and the unit-ball rays transported
as specified above. In particular, it is not an arbitrary operator with smooth coefficients. Locally u is
Lipschitz and X is C 1,1 . The complementarity relations (7.13) hold pointwise.
Proof. For a compactly supported interior variation K ′ = p, the coefficient after integration by
parts is                    [                                 ]
                           2 u(τ g − K) − sym ∇X + (div X)g : p.                            (7.19)
Its trace gives div X = −uτ , and its trace-free part then gives (7.17). For metric tests the scalar-
curvature principal adjoint is Hess u − (∆u)g. Connection variations in the momentum term give
smooth linear expressions in u, X, ∇X; fixed-flux field terms and the ray-transport term are algebraic
in those moments. The area term has coefficient −c(g − νF♭ ⊗ νF♭ )/2 on this side of (7.11). Inverting
trace reversal gives (7.18), with the displayed positive normal-normal measure.
     Differentiating (7.17) and commuting covariant derivatives expresses every component of ∇2 X
as a smooth linear combination of Rm ∗X, K ∗ du and (∇K) ∗ u. Taking traces gives a strongly
elliptic Laplace system for (u, X) with smooth lower-order coefficients. Its source measure has local
growth O(rn−1 ), uniformly in the minimizer, by the local perimeter bound. A localized elliptic
parametrix has order −2 and kernel bounds C|x − y|2−n−j for j = 0, 1. Such a potential of a measure
with this growth is locally C γ for every 0 < γ < 1: split the potential difference at twice the point
separation, use the growth bound nearby and the first kernel derivative on successive outer annuli
to get O(r(1 + | log r|)). Lower-order terms are included in the parametrix, and the homogeneous
remainder is smooth. Thus u, X are Hölder continuous.
    The traced differentiated equation for X now has the form of the divergence of a Hölder field
plus a Hölder function; local elliptic estimates give X ∈ C 1,γ , after decreasing γ if needed. The lower

                                                   58
term in (7.18) is therefore continuous. Its positive measure term implies a local lower bound for
the covariant Hessian in the distributional sense. Slice this inequality in smooth flow boxes whose
longitudinal curves are unit-speed geodesics. Multiplying by the smooth volume Jacobian gives the
one-dimensional distributional inequality (u ◦ γ)′′ ≥ −C on almost every such curve. Continuity
extends its integrated convexity inequality to every curve in a smaller box. This is local geodesic
semiconvexity, which implies a local Lipschitz bound by bounding one-sided slopes on short geodesic
segments with endpoints in a fixed larger ball. The differentiated equation for X consequently has
bounded right-hand side componentwise, so ∇2 X is locally bounded and X ∈ C 1,1 . The measure
inequalities now reduce to pointwise inequalities by continuity.

   For clarity about the field coefficients in this adjoint, holding α = iE volg fixed gives E ′ =
−(tr h)E/2 and hence

                 δ 2uc2n |E|2 volg = uc2n 2Ei Ej − |E|2 gij hij volg = −uSij hij volg .
                  (              )       (                 )
                                                                                                 (7.20)

The ray transport contributes −(Jm )(i Xj) hij . The added variation of volume in the full constraint
pairing is zero by complementarity. These identities, together with the actual gravitational adjoint,
will control null focusing in the next section. In particular, the singular term in (7.18) cannot be
discarded by assuming regularity of a minimizing frontier.


8    Eliminating interior area multipliers
We prove that the area multiplier obtained from separation is concentrated on the given boundary.
The main issue is that an area minimizer can have a singular frontier in high dimension. Atomic
transverse mass forces a regular sheet to be totally geodesic. For the remaining sheets, a common
positive Jacobi field, null focusing, and a cone argument give a uniform curvature bound. A separate
locality argument removes diffuse multiplier mass from sheets on which the lapse vanishes. All these
arguments concern the original smooth data.
    Throughout this section, k = n − 1, and F∗ and π are the compact family and probability
measure of Proposition 7.1 and equation 7.11. We use the full-measure family supplied by Lemma 7.3,
intersected with supp π. A member of this family has frontier S, or has compact frontier wholly in
the open exterior and satisfies
                                         HΣ = 0,      trΣ K = 0                                 (8.1)
on every free regular sheet. We retain the local common-sheet, noncrossing, and graphical compact-
ness conclusions of Proposition 7.1. In particular, distinct nearby sheets are disjoint graphs, and on
smaller patches the tilt between them is bounded by a constant times their separation.
   The moments of Proposition 7.4 satisfy
                                                                    c
                                                                        ∫
                                                                              ♭    ♭
    sym ∇X = −uK,          ∇2 u = B(u, X, ∇X) + M,             M=            νD ⊗ νD dAD dπ(D)    (8.2)
                                                                    2   F∗

in the open exterior. Here c > 0, u is locally Lipschitz, X is locally C 1,1 , and the last formula
defines a tensor-valued measure relative to the smooth volume density. Its singular sets have zero
sheet area. Here B abbreviates the operator L of Proposition 7.4. It is the precise lower-order
metric-adjoint expression in equation 7.11, with the electric flux held fixed and the unit-ball rays
transported isometrically. It has smooth coefficients and is linear in its displayed arguments. Its
origin, and not merely its regularity, will be used in the focusing calculation. We also have

                         u ≥ |X|,       µm ≥ |Jm |,      uµm + Jm (X) = 0.                        (8.3)

                                                  59
8.1   Local leaf measures and atomic sheets
Fix a free regular incidence point (D, p). The uniform graph conclusion of Proposition 7.1, after
shrinking its neighborhood, provides nested open spatial boxes

                                 p ∈ W ⋐ V ⋐ U,        W = B0 × I0 ,

in coordinates (y, t), and a larger base ball B1 ⊃ B0 , with the following property. Every minimizing
frontier that meets V has in U a unique selected graph t = fF (y) over B1 ; all portions of that
frontier in W belong to this graph. Its graph over B1 lies in the coordinate chart, and the graphs
have uniform interior smooth bounds and are mutually disjoint unless they coincide. The boxes can
be chosen with this property by first taking the uniform graph neighborhood and then choosing the
middle and inner boxes with positive margin from its side and top faces.
    Put
                                HV = {F ∈ F∗ : frontier(F ) ∩ V ̸= ∅}.
This is an open subset of the minimizing family. Indeed convergence in that compact family is strict
perimeter convergence, hence its perimeter measures converge weakly. If the limit frontier meets
the open set V , its perimeter measure is positive on a nonnegative compactly supported test in V ,
and the same holds for every sufficiently late member of the sequence. This proves openness. The
uniform density bounds also give local Hausdorff convergence of the frontier supports: a putative
boundary point staying a positive distance from the limit frontier would force positive volumes of
both phases in a ball where the limit is constant, contradicting local L1 convergence.
    The graph map
                               qV : HV −→ C ∞ (B1 ),     qV (F ) = fF ,
is continuous for the topology of smooth convergence on compact subsets. To see this, apply regular
multiplicity-one convergence at the limiting graph on each compact subball; the uniform graph
bounds and uniqueness identify every subsequential limit. Define the finite Borel measure

                                          λV = (qV )∗ (π|HV )

on the entire separable metrizable function space C ∞ (B1 ). This definition identifies coincident local
graphs without requiring the image of qV to be a Borel subset of that space. We write G for this
leaf-parameter space and λ for λV when the box is fixed.
    Every source contribution on W is retained: a frontier meeting W also meets V , and is therefore
in HV . No arbitrary neighborhood of the reference minimizer is used to truncate this measure.
Distinct realized graph parameters are disjoint, so for each y ∈ B1 the map

                                              ℓ ⟼ fℓ (y)                                          (8.4)

is injective on the realized leaves. All integrals with respect to λ mean the corresponding pushforward
integrals over HV ; the injectivity assertion concerns the realized graphs in those integrals.
Lemma 8.1 (Atomic leaves). If λ({ℓ0 }) > 0, the corresponding regular sheet is totally geodesic on
graph(fℓ0 ) ∩ W .
Proof. Fix any point of graph(fℓ0 ) ∩ W and take signed normal-distance coordinates (y, s) about
that sheet on a smaller patch compactly contained in W , writing the sheet as Σ = {s = 0}. Put
m0 = λ({ℓ0 }) > 0. The ss equation in (8.2) is a one-dimensional second-derivative equation after
slicing by almost every normal line. The bounded nonmeasure terms include B and the coordinate
connection terms. The atom at s = 0 has coefficient cm0 /2: on the reference graph the unit conormal

                                                  60
is ds and the sheet and volume Jacobians agree at s = 0. No other graph produces an atom there,
by (8.4). Consequently ∂s u has bounded one-sided traces v ± (y) for almost every y, and
                                                          c
                                       v + (y) − v − (y) = m0 .                                  (8.5)
                                                          2
The existence and boundedness of these traces can also be read directly from local semiconvexity of
u along the normal lines.
   We compare the tangential equations from the two sides. Let As be the second fundamental
form of the parallel sheet {s = constant}, with normal ∂s . With As (V, W ) = g(∇V ∂s , W ),

                                  (∇2 u)|T Σs = ∇2Σs (u|Σs ) + As ∂s u.                          (8.6)

Average this identity on each of 0 < s < ε and −ε < s < 0, pairing with any smooth compactly
supported tangential tensor in the y variables. The intrinsic Hessian terms have the same limit:
integrate twice in y, then use uniform convergence of u(y, s) to u(y, 0) and smooth convergence of
the parallel metrics. The B terms also have the same limit, since u, X, ∇X are continuous.
    The averaged tangential components of the sheet measure tend to zero. Indeed the conormal of
a nearby graph has tangential component bounded by C|s| relative to Σ at a point of height s. This
is the graph-difference estimate recalled above, with the smooth change from graph coordinates to
normal coordinates included. Thus in a slab of width ε the tangential-tangential source is bounded
in total variation by Cε2 times the total sheet measure in a fixed box. Dividing by ε still gives a
quantity tending to zero. The atom on Σ itself has no tangential component.
    Subtracting the two limits in (8.6) gives A0 (v + − v − ) = 0 as a tensor distribution on Σ.
Equation (8.5) and cm0 > 0 imply A0 = 0. The sheet is smooth, so the conclusion holds at every
point of the patch.

Lemma 8.2 (A common Jacobi field). Let D belong to the full-measure family above, and let Σ be a
connected component of its regular interior frontier. Suppose one of its graph patches is a nonatom
in the support of the corresponding leaf measure. Then there is a smooth function φ > 0 on Σ such
that
                                      L0 φ = L+ φ = L− φ = 0.                                  (8.7)
Here L0 , L+ , and L− are the outward normal linearizations of H, H + trtan K, and H − trtan K,
respectively, at Σ. All have principal part −∆Σ , and

                                 L0 = −∆Σ − |AΣ |2 + Ricg (ν, ν) .
                                                (                    )
                                                                                                 (8.8)

Proof. Choose a base point p in the specified patch. Since its graph parameter is a nonatom in
the support of the leaf measure, choose distinct realized graphs converging smoothly on compact
subpatches to the reference graph, with representatives Dj in the full-measure family. Such
representatives exist because every graph neighborhood has positive pushforward measure, whereas
the reference fiber has measure zero. Compactness of F∗ permits passing to a subsequence with
Dj → D′ for some minimizing filled set D′ . We do not assert that D′ = D away from the selected
component.
   The limiting frontier of D′ contains the reference graph near p. Indeed points on the converging
graphs are frontier points of Dj , and uniform density and local L1 convergence put their limits on
the frontier of D′ . Set
                                        C = Σ ∩ frontier(D′ ).
This set is nonempty and relatively closed in Σ. It is also relatively open: at each of its points the
reference frontier is free and regular, and the common-sheet property of Proposition 7.1 makes the

                                                    61
frontier of D′ agree with it in a neighborhood. Connectedness gives C = Σ. In particular every
point of Σ is a regular point of the limiting frontier D′ .
     Choose a nested exhaustion of Σ by connected precompact smooth domains Ωm containing p,
with Ωm ⊂ Ωm+1 ; if Σ is compact, use Σ itself. Use the fixed normal of Σ and its normal exponential
map on a tubular neighborhood of each compact stage. Regular multiplicity-one convergence to D′
gives, for sufficiently large j at that stage, a unique normal height hj over Ωm . A finite covering of
the slightly larger compact stage by regular graph patches supplies this assertion uniformly on Ωm .
Uniqueness on each normal fiber makes the heights agree on overlaps and on successive exhaustion
stages. Thus there is no choice of branch or monodromy in this construction.
     The heights have a strict constant sign on each connected stage. If hj vanished at a point, the
common-sheet property would make its zero set relatively open as well as closed there; connectedness
would force coincidence at p, contrary to the distinct initial graphs. Passing to a subsequence fixes
the sign at p, and hence on every exhaustion stage. Replace hj by −hj in the linear difference
equations if that sign is negative, and normalize by hj (p) > 0. The equations H = 0 and trtan K = 0
are unaffected by choosing the fixed graph normal, even if a representative filled set induces the
opposite orientation on its local graph. Subtract the three graph equations (8.1). The integral
of the linearization along the segment between the two graphs gives linear elliptic equations for
hj , whose coefficients converge smoothly on compact subsets to those of L0 , L+ , L− . Harnack’s
inequality and interior elliptic estimates applied to the first equation bound hj /hj (p) above and
below, with all derivatives, on each exhaustion stage. A diagonal subsequence converges smoothly
to φ, with φ(p) = 1. Harnack’s inequality gives positivity. Passing to the limit in all three equations
proves (8.7). The same argument applies when the approach is from the other side, since all three
linearized equations are homogeneous.

    The support qualification in this lemma loses no multiplier mass. On each graph box the
complement of the support of λ has zero λ-measure. A countable collection of such boxes suffices
for the regular incidence set, as explained below.

8.2   Focusing with a measure-valued Hessian
Lemma 8.3 (Weak focusing). On a free regular sheet satisfying (8.1), define

                             Q+ = u − g(X, ν),          Q− = u + g(X, ν).                        (8.9)

These functions are nonnegative and locally Lipschitz. On the open part of the sheet where u > 0,
they satisfy the distributional inequalities

                                      L± Q± ≤ −u|AΣ ± Ktan |2 .                                 (8.10)

Proof. We first identify the sign of the singular term in the stationary curvature calculation. For
smooth positive u and smooth X, set

                g = −u2 dz 2 + gij (dxi + X i dz)(dxj + X j dz),    n = u−1 (∂z − X).           (8.11)

The second fundamental form of a stationary slice is b = − sym ∇X/u, with the convention for K
used in the theorem. Define B(P, Q) = P : Q − (tr P )(tr Q). Integrating the momentum term in the
gravitational constraint pairing at fixed lapse and shift gives the algebraic expression
                  (                           )     (                               )
                u Rg − B(K, K) + 2B(K, b) = u Rg + B(b, b) − B(K − b, K − b) .                  (8.12)



                                                   62
At b = K its first metric variation agrees with that of the stationary scalar-curvature action, up to
divergences. The spatial metric coefficient of that variation is −uGij , where G is the Einstein tensor
of (8.11). This identity can equally be obtained by substituting b = − sym ∇X/u and integrating
the derivatives of a compactly supported metric variation; it uses no field equation.
    Let S be the Maxwell stress of a two-form whose slice electric field is cn E and whose slice
magnetic part is zero. Only these slice values are needed here. Holding the electric flux form fixed
under g ′ = h gives

                   δ 2uc2n |E|2 dVg = uc2n (2Ei Ej − |E|2 gij )hij dVg = −uSij hij dVg .

Adding the variation of the volume density in the full matter pairing does not alter the adjoint
coefficient, by uµm + Jm (X) = 0. Transport of a unit-ball ray contributes −(Jm )(i Xj) , where
parentheses include the factor 1/2. It follows from the actual metric-adjoint expression in (8.2) that
a Hessian source M contributes
                                                              (                    )
                             u(G − S)ij = −(Jm )(i Xj) + (trg M )g − M ij .                            (8.14)

For a positive semidefinite M , the last tensor is positive semidefinite: for a unit vector v, complete
v to an orthonormal basis and sum M on the remaining basis vectors. In particular the sign is
preserved by trace reversal in precisely this direction.
   The lower Hessian term can be read off explicitly. Write τb = trg b and (b2 )ij = bi l blj . The
spatial stationary Einstein identity is

                  uGij = (∆g u)gij − (∇2g u)ij + (Cb )ij ,                                             (8.15)
                             [                                             ]
                     Cb = u Ricg +τb b − 2b2 − 12 (Rg + τb2 + |b|2 )g − LX b + X(τb )g.

For the mollified pair, the same identity with b = bϵ shows that C 1 convergence of bϵ controls every
non-Hessian spatial term.
    We justify the use of this calculation at the regularity of (8.2). Fix relatively compact coordinate
patches U ′ ⋐ U ⋐ {u > 0}, and choose a > 0 with u ≥ a on U . All smoothing below is performed
in U , then restricted to U ′ . Let ρϵ be a nonnegative standard mollifier, and convolve the coordinate
components of u and β = X ♭ : uϵ = u ∗ ρϵ , βϵ = β ∗ ρϵ , Xϵ = g −1 βϵ . The spatial metric is not
changed. In components the first equation of (8.2) reads ∂(i βj) = Γlij βl − uKij . Hence the residual

                     Rϵ := sym ∇βϵ + uϵ K = (Γβ) ∗ ρϵ − Γβϵ − (uK) ∗ ρϵ + uϵ K                         (8.17)

                                               (                                                  )
       ∥(Af ) ∗ ρϵ − A(f ∗ ρϵ )∥C 1 (U ′ ) ≤ Cϵ ∥A∥C 2 (U ) ∥f ∥L∞ (U ) + ∥A∥C 1 (U ) ∥Df ∥L∞ (U ) .   (8.18)

For its derivative, differentiate the product to obtain the sum of the commutator of DA with f and

                         bϵ := − sym ∇Xϵ /uϵ = K − Rϵ /uϵ −→ K                 in C 1 (U ′ ).          (8.19)

    In the Hessian equation, first express the measure-valued components relative to coordinate

this already un-trace-reversed equation. It gives

              ∇2g uϵ = Bϵ + Mϵ + (Γ du) ∗ ρϵ − Γ duϵ ,       Bϵ −→ B      uniformly on U ′ .        (8.20)

The connection commutator is O(ϵ) in C 0 because du is bounded. For every x, Mϵ (x) is a positive
semidefinite matrix. We trace this matrix using g(x) only after convolution. Consequently

                              Pϵ (x) := (trg(x) Mϵ (x))g(x) − Mϵ (x) ≥ 0                            (8.21)

exactly, even if its norm diverges as ϵ ↓ 0. There is no product commutator involving a variable
metric times an unbounded singular Hessian.
     Apply the smooth stationary calculation to uϵ , Xϵ and bϵ . Formula (8.19) controls the induced
constraint densities and all the non-Hessian spatial adjoint terms uniformly. Indeed (8.15) uses only
bϵ , ∇bϵ , uϵ , Xϵ , ∇Xϵ outside the Hessian term. Equation (8.16) shows that uniform convergence of
duϵ is unnecessary. Writing Tϵ = Gϵ − Sϵ , we obtain uniformly on U ′

           Tϵ (nϵ , nϵ ) = µm + o(1),                              Tϵ (nϵ , ·)|T U ′ = Jm + o(1),   (8.22)
             uϵ (Tϵ )ij = −(Jm )(i Xj) + (Pϵ )ij + o(1).

To see the null sign of the bounded terms, when µm > 0, (7.13) forces |X| = u and Jm = −µm X ♭ /u.
Their limiting block tensor is µm ℓ ⊗ ℓ, with ℓ(n) = 1 and ℓ(V ) = −g(X, V )/u. When µm = 0, all its
blocks vanish. This description, or the direct formula uµm (1 ∓ g(X, ν)/u)2 , proves nonnegativity on
n ± ν. Maxwell stress is also nonnegative on null vectors. The exact positivity of Pϵ therefore gives

                                     uϵ Ricgϵ (nϵ ± ν, nϵ ± ν) ≥ −o(1).                             (8.23)

This is a one-sided estimate; it requires no uniform bound on the full spacetime curvature.
    For completeness, apply the null shape-operator equation to the outgoing null geodesics orthog-
onal to the fixed sheet in the smooth metric gϵ . Use nearby stationary slices as the parameter
surfaces. At the initial slice the null velocity with dz/dτ = 1 is uϵ (nϵ + ν). Subtracting the stationary
translation ∂z leaves the spatial velocity uϵ ν − Xϵ , whose normal component is uϵ − g(Xϵ , ν). The
Riccati equation gives the expansion derivative

                             −uϵ |AΣ + (bϵ )tan |2 + Ricgϵ (nϵ + ν, nϵ + ν) ,
                                 (                                          )
                                                                                                    (8.24)

up to a bounded renormalization factor times the initial expansion. Tangential transport contributes
a bounded tangential velocity times the tangential derivative of that expansion. Both error terms
tend to zero uniformly: H + trΣ K = 0 and bϵ → K in C 1 . The spatial expansion linearization
has coefficients converging uniformly to those of L+ . Its principal part is always the fixed −∆Σ ;
convergence of its action on uϵ − g(Xϵ , ν) therefore holds in distributions, using uniform convergence
of these functions and uniform bounds on their first derivatives. Combining (8.23)–(8.24) proves
the plus inequality in (8.10). Replace the future normal by −nϵ , equivalently reverse stationary
time and Xϵ , bϵ , to obtain the minus inequality. Since U ′ ⋐ {u > 0} was arbitrary, this proves the
stated local result.

8.3   Positive Jacobi fields at singular ends
We give the analytic details needed at singular points. Their purpose is stronger than removability
of a bounded subsolution: a bounded reciprocal Jacobi field must tend to zero at every singular end.

Lemma 8.4 (Capacity and a local mean-value estimate). Let T be a multiplicity-one locally
perimeter-minimizing hypersurface in a compact smooth ambient patch, of dimension k ≥ 3. Its
singular set has zero surface 2-capacity. A bounded nonnegative continuous function w on the
regular part, satisfying ∆T w ≥ −C0 there, extends weakly across the singular set and satisfies, for
sufficiently small ambient balls centered on supp T ,

component and extended by zero on the other components.
Proof. The singular dimension bound is k − 7, and local perimeter bounds give Area(T ∩ Bρ ) ≤ Cρk ;
see [25]. If the singular set is nonempty, cover a compact part of it by balls Bri with ri arbitrarily

perimeter bound. Their scaled versions remain bounded as r ↓ 0.
Lemma 8.5 (A bounded cone subsolution vanishes). Let C ⊂ Rk+1 be a nonflat, multiplicity-one,
perimeter-minimizing boundary cone. A bounded nonnegative continuous function z on its entire
regular part satisfying
                                       ∆C z ≥ |AC |2 z                                 (8.29)
in distributions is identically zero. The function may be zero on some regular components.

                                                                65
Proof. Only k ≥ 7 can occur. Let L = C ∩ Sk be the link, let s = log r, and put V = |AL |2 . The
inequality becomes
                                 zss + (k − 2)zs + ∆L z ≥ V z.                             (8.30)
We first justify testing across the singular link.∫ On each compact axial slab, the Caccioppoli test

                                                q ′′ + (k − 2)q ′ ≥ 0                               (8.31)

q ′ (s) ≤ q ′ (s0 )e(k−2)(s0 −s) . Integrating backward contradicts boundedness. Thus every convolution is
nondecreasing, and the original q has a nondecreasing representative. If z is nonzero, its forward
limit q∞ is strictly positive.

are bounded uniformly, and their integrals tend to q∞ . Take a weak-star convergent subsequence in

parts in s bounds the axial terms by ∥z∥∞ ∥ψ∥L1 (L) (∥wT′′ ∥1 + (k − 2)∥wT′ ∥1 ), which tends to zero.

                               ∆L z ≥ V z,         z dAL = q∞ > 0.                              (8.33)

Bounded subsolution regularity and the capacity argument give the needed local weak derivatives.

                        1
                            ∫                   ∫                        ∫
                                χ2j |∇L z|2 +       V χ2j z 2 ≤ 2∥z∥2∞       |∇L χj |2 −→ 0.        (8.34)
                        2   L                   L                        L

Thus z is constant on every connected regular component of L, and a positive constant can occur
only on a totally geodesic component.
    We show that a nonflat minimizing boundary cone has no such component. A connected totally
geodesic regular component lies in an equator Q ∼     = Sk−1 . It is relatively open there; its relative
boundary lies in the singular set, since at a regular boundary point the same smooth sheet continues.
A closed set of Hausdorff dimension less than (k − 1) − 1 does not disconnect Q: in coordinate
balls one connects points by polygonal paths whose vertices are chosen outside the measure-zero
exceptional sets of segments meeting that set, and then concatenates such paths. Here the singular
set has dimension at most k − 8. It follows that this component contains Q minus the singular set,
and its closure contains the entire equator.
    Any other regular component lies strictly in one open hemisphere determined by Q. It cannot
meet Q at a regular point without being part of the same regular sheet. On that component the
signed coordinate height h is strictly of one sign and satisfies ∆L h = −(k − 1)h. It and its gradient
are bounded. Integrate against capacity cutoffs, extended by zero on other components. The

                                                            66
                                                        ∫
boundary terms tend to zero by Cauchy–Schwarz; hence h = 0, a contradiction. There are no other
regular components. Regular points are dense in the support, and multiplicity one now says that C
is the plane through Q. The componentwise interpretation is also consistent with the decomposition
of a multiplicity-one oriented minimizing boundary into minimizing regular components; see [25,
Chapter 7, Section 5, Corollary 5.11 and Remark 5.12]. This contradicts nonflatness. Therefore
(8.33) is impossible and z = 0.

Lemma 8.6 (Reciprocal Jacobi decay). Let Σ be a connected regular component of a compact
interior minimizing frontier, and let φ > 0 solve (8.8). Then

               inf φ > 0,       φ(x) −→ +∞    as x ∈ Σ approaches the singular set.           (8.35)
                Σ

The second assertion is vacuous for a smooth compact component.
Proof. The Ricci curvature is bounded on a compact neighborhood of the frontier; choose C with
Ricg (ν, ν) ≥ −C. For δ > 0 put wδ = (1 − φ/δ)+ on Σ, and extend it by zero on other regular
components. Convex truncation of the Jacobi equation gives ∆wδ ≥ −C; on {φ < δ} this follows
from −∆φ/δ = (|A|2 + Ric(ν, ν))φ/δ ≥ −C, and the truncation contributes a nonnegative measure.
Moreover 0 ≤ wδ ≤ 1 and wδ → 0 almost everywhere as δ ↓ 0.
    Choose first a fixed radius r > 0 so small that the Cr2 term in (8.25), including its constant,
is less than 1/4. Cover the compact frontier by finitely many balls of radius r/2. Dominated
convergence in their radius-r enlargements then permits choosing δ > 0 so that the integral term is
less than 1/4 in every ball. Thus wδ ≤ 1/2 on Σ, and φ ≥ δ/2. This proves the first assertion with
the required order of choices, r before δ.
    Set z = 1/φ and extend it by zero to the other regular components. It is bounded and satisfies

                    ∆Σ z = |A|2 + Ricg (ν, ν) z + 2φ−3 |∇Σ φ|2 ≥ (|A|2 − C)z.
                            (                 )
                                                                                              (8.36)

Fix a singular point p and any sequence of scales rj ↓ 0. Pass to a subsequence along which the
rescaled frontiers converge to a multiplicity-one minimizing tangent cone Cp . The cone is nonflat:
a multiplicity-one planar tangent cone would imply regularity of the frontier at p. These are the
standard compactness and excess-regularity conclusions for minimizing boundaries [25].
    On compact subsets of the regular cone the convergence is smooth. The rescaled z have
subsequences converging locally uniformly to a bounded nonnegative function z0 . Here is a useful
precise way to obtain this convergence. On a connected regular patch, either z tends to zero at
one base point, in which case Harnack for the positive Jacobi function φ forces z → 0 on smaller
patches, or z stays bounded below there along a subsequence. In the latter case the same Harnack
inequality and interior estimates bound φ and its derivatives, giving smooth convergence of z. The
uniform upper bound for z was already established. Branches on which the zero extension is used
cause no difficulty; choose the branch in each regular patch and diagonalize. Thus one obtains z0 on
the whole regular cone, allowing zero components. Scaling (8.36) and passing to regular patches
gives ∆Cp z0 ≥ |ACp |2 z0 . Lemma 8.5 implies z0 = 0.
    This regular convergence implies vanishing of the normalized L2 integral of z on each fixed
bounded rescaled ball. To check points near the singular cone, first discard a small neighborhood of
that set and of the cone vertex. Their limiting area can be made arbitrarily small. Area convergence
and the uniform bound for z control the discarded part, while smooth regular convergence controls
its complement. The scaled version of (8.25), with source bounded by Crj2 ∥z∥∞ , now gives uniform
decay on smaller balls.
    In particular, if xj ∈ Σ tends to p, choose scales rj comparable to dg (xj , p). The preceding
argument and the mean-value estimate show z(xj ) → 0; any contrary subsequence would have a

                                                  67
tangent-cone subsequence of the type just treated. A sequence approaching the compact singular
set has a subsequence tending to a particular singular point. Applying this fixed-point argument to
that subsequence proves the uniform sequential assertion in (8.35).

8.4    Diffuse mass at a zero lapse and the final curvature bound
Lemma 8.7 (No diffuse leaf mass on the zero set). In a uniform graph box, let λna denote the
nonatomic part of its leaf measure. Let

                     ZW = {ℓ : graph(fℓ ) ∩ W ̸= ∅, u = 0 on graph(fℓ ) ∩ W }.

Then
                                               λna (ZW ) = 0.                                    (8.37)

Proof. On W = B0 × I0 , the coordinate tt equation in (8.2) is the following equality of measures:
                                                  ∫
                     ∂t2 u = b(y, t) dy dt + dy       w(ℓ, y)1I0 (fℓ (y))δfℓ (y) (dt) dλ(ℓ).     (8.38)
                                                  G

Here y ∈ B0 and the left side and bounded-density term are restricted to W as measures. The
indicator records the restriction of the graph measure to the working t-interval; it is not a differ-
entiation of a characteristic function. The density b is bounded, and 0 < w0 ≤ w(ℓ, y) ≤ w1 for
the contributing graphs. The weight is c/2 times the squared conormal on ∂t , times the graph
area Jacobian, divided by the volume Jacobian. All contributions on W are included because their
frontiers lie in HV . Pairing with product tests and using the graph area formula proves the display;
measure approximation then gives its linewise disintegration.
    For almost every y, t ↦ u(y, t) is Lipschitz and its derivative vy = ∂t u(y, ·) is a one-dimensional
BV function. This follows from the sliced equation, or from semiconvexity and Fubini. Write
Zy = {t : u(y, t) = 0}. At any point of Zy where the two one-sided precise values of vy agree, their
common value is zero: a nonzero common value would contradict the nonnegative minimum of the
primitive u(y, ·) at that point. The diffuse derivative of a one-dimensional BV function does not
charge a fixed precise-value level. In the usual Da v + Dc v notation this locality statement is

                                       |Da v + Dc v| {ṽ = 0} = 0;
                                                        (         )
                                                                                                 (8.39)

it follows from the BV chain rule, or directly from the one-dimensional coarea formula; see [2,
Proposition 8, equation (16)]. Consequently the diffuse part of Dvy gives no mass to Zy . The
countably many jump points do not affect that assertion.
    The bounded term b is zero almost everywhere on {u = 0}. Indeed X = 0 there by u ≥ |X|, and
the first derivatives of u and X vanish almost everywhere on their respective zero sets. Thus the
linear expression B(u, X, ∇X) and the connection term involving du both vanish almost everywhere
there. Fubini gives b(y, ·) = 0 almost everywhere on Zy for almost every line.
    Finally, by (8.4) the pushforward of w(ℓ, y)dλna (ℓ) under ℓ ↦ fℓ (y) is atomless for every y. All
line atoms of the area source in (8.38) therefore come from transverse atoms of λ; there are no
hidden atoms produced by the evaluation map. On almost every line, restrict the diffuse part of
(8.38) to Zy and use (8.39) and the preceding vanishing of b. It follows that
                            ∫
                                 w(ℓ, y)1I0 (fℓ (y))1{u(y,fℓ (y))=0} dλna (ℓ) = 0                (8.40)
                             G

                                                                        / I0 , so u is evaluated only
inside W . For each leaf set              ∫

Proposition 8.8 (Elimination of the interior area multiplier). Under the hypotheses above and the
exclusion of a smooth interior enclosing frontier with zero future expansion in Definition 2.6, the
measure π is concentrated on the given boundary:
                                               ∫
                               π = δS ,             a′D (h) dπ(D) = a′S (h).                    (8.41)
                                               F∗

Here δS denotes the point mass at the filled minimizer whose frontier is S.

Proof. First consider a connected regular component Σ with a nonatomic support patch. Choose the
common field φ of Lemma 8.2. The functions Q± of (8.9) are bounded on the compact frontier. By
Lemma 8.6, Q± /φ tends to zero at every singular end. If one of these ratios is positive somewhere,
it therefore attains a positive maximum at a regular interior point. This is also immediate when Σ
is smooth and compact.
    Write Q± = φv. For an operator L = −∆ + b · ∇ + d with Lφ = 0, direct product differentiation
gives
                                L(φv) = φ [−∆v + (b − 2∇ log φ) · ∇v] .                      (8.42)
At any point where Q± > 0, one has u > 0, so Lemma 8.3 applies. The weak strong maximum
principle for the locally Lipschitz v and the smooth coefficients in (8.42) implies that v is constant
at its positive maximum on the connected component of {Q± > 0} containing that point. This
component is also closed in Σ: at a regular limit point, Q± = vφ > 0 by continuity. Since Σ is
connected, it is all of Σ. Substitution in (8.10) then gives

                                  AΣ ± Ktan = 0,           |AΣ | ≤ |K|.                         (8.43)

The weak maximum principle used here is the ordinary local elliptic one, for example [13]; no
maximum principle on an unresolved singular boundary is used. If neither ratio is positive anywhere,
Q+ = Q− = 0 on Σ, hence u = 0 there and X = 0 there by domination. Lemma 8.7 excludes
multiplier mass on this alternative in every nonatomic patch. In atomic patches Lemma 8.1 already
gives A = 0.
    We make the exceptional set independent of the regular incidence point. The open inner boxes
W constructed above cover all free regular points of all minimizing frontiers. Their union is an open
subset of the second-countable ambient manifold, so choose a countable subcover Wi , retaining the
corresponding middle boxes Vi and graph maps qi on the open families HVi . In particular the open
sets HVi × Wi cover the regular incidence set in the family-times-manifold topology.
    For each i let λi = (qi )∗ (π|HVi ), let Ai be its countable set of atoms, and let Zi = ZWi be the
zero-leaf set from Lemma 8.7. These are measurable sets. For the last assertion, the condition that


                                                     69
a graph meets Wi is open in the graph topology. The condition that u vanishes on its intersection
with Wi can be checked at a countable dense set of base points: failure gives, by continuity, an open
base set on which the graph stays inside Wi and u > 0. Thus it is a Borel condition.
   The set
                              Ni = C ∞ (B1,i ) \ supp λi ∪ (Zi \ Ai )
                                    (                   )

has λi -measure zero, since the second term has λi -measure λna
                                                             i (Zi ) = 0. Consequently
                                    (                            )
                                   π {F ∈ HVi : qi (F ) ∈ Ni } = 0.

Remove the countable union of these pullbacks, together with the already excluded null set in the
good family. For a remaining frontier and any of its regular points p, choose i with p ∈ Wi . Its leaf
is either an atom, giving A = 0 locally, or a nonatom in the support of λi . In the latter case the
common Jacobi argument applies on its entire connected regular component. If that component had
u = 0 everywhere, the leaf would belong to Zi \ Ai , which was removed. Therefore the positive-ratio
alternative gives |A| ≤ |K|. This proves the curvature bound simultaneously at every regular point
of every remaining frontier. In particular,

                                             |A| ≤ sup |K|,                                      (8.44)
                                                     K

where K is a common compact set containing the minimizing frontiers.
    Fix such an interior frontier and any proposed singular point. Blow it up at scales tending to
zero. The tangent cone is a multiplicity-one minimizing boundary cone, with smooth convergence
on its regular part. The rescaled bound (8.44) tends to zero, so every regular component of the
cone is totally geodesic. The equator and height argument in Lemma 8.5 shows that the cone is a
plane. Multiplicity-one planar tangent regularity contradicts the point being singular. The entire
compact frontier is therefore smooth.
    This frontier is a genuine enclosing cut. In particular the minimizing filled set has no removable
bounded open component of its complement: filling one would preserve the obstacle condition and
strictly reduce its full perimeter. Its components carry the outward normals of that enclosing set,
and (8.1) gives zero future expansion on every component. The assumed exclusion of a smooth
interior enclosing marginal frontier rules it out. Consequently π-almost every frontier is S. The filled
set with frontier S and the prescribed obstacle is unique modulo null sets, and π is a probability.
This proves (8.41).

8.5   Smooth stationarity and the boundary laws
We finish the original-data variation argument. The lapse u and shift X in the following proposition
are the measure moments constructed above; they are unrelated to the lapse notation used locally
in the filled scalar construction.

Proposition 8.9 (Stationary data supplied by equality). There are smooth fields u, X on Ω,
including S, with u ≥ |X|, u > 0 in the open exterior and

                              sym ∇X = −uK,              uµm + Jm (X) = 0.                       (8.45)

For any sufficiently small decrease q0 < q with q0 > (n − 2)/2, their end behavior is

                                     (u, X) = (b0 , bi ) + O2 (r−q0 ).                           (8.46)

On Rz × int Ω set

        g = −u2 dz 2 + gij (dxi + X i dz)(dxj + X j dz),             ξ = ∂z ,       F = cn dz ∧ uE ♭ .   (8.47)

The stationary fields satisfy Maxwell’s equations and
                                                                 (                           )
                    Eing −S = µm u−2 ξ ♭ ⊗ ξ ♭ ,       Sab = 2 Fac Fb c − 14 Fcd Fcd gab .               (8.48)

The extra term is zero unless ξ is null. The original slice has future normal N = (∂z − X)/u and
induces exactly (g, K, E), with F restricting to zero on it. There is a global smooth function p,
including up to S, with

                dp = (k − 1)uE ♭ ,       p(∞) = 0,          p = O2 (r2−n ),           d(iX α) = 0.       (8.49)

   Let κ = c/2 > 0. On S the boundary laws are

                          X = uν,        ∂ν u + K(X, ν) = κ,              cH = 2L∗+ u,                   (8.50)

where L+ is the outward normal linearization of H + trS K, and its adjoint uses dAg . Either u > 0
on all of S, or u = 0 on all of S. The nonnegative function W = u2 − |X|2 tends to one at infinity,
vanishes on S, and is strictly positive in a deleted collar of S. If u > 0 on S, then

                                             dW = 2κX ♭      on S.                                       (8.51)

If u = 0 on S, writing s for exterior distance from S, there are smooth fields a∗ , Y∗ such that

                                 u = sa∗ ,         X = s2 Y∗ ,         a∗ |S = κ.                        (8.52)

No assertion of a Lorentzian extension across the zero-lapse boundary is made at this stage.
Proof. Smoothness and boundary moments. Proposition 8.8 removes the interior surface measure
from (7.18). Together with the differentiated KID equation, the resulting equations express all
second derivatives of u, X as smooth linear expressions in their first jets. Ordinary elliptic regularity
makes them smooth in the open exterior. The full first-jet system is closed; along the normals of a
smooth collar it is a linear ODE with smooth coefficients. Starting on an interior collar face, solve
that ODE to S. Smooth dependence on the tangential parameters gives smooth extension of all
fields and their jets. This argument also shows that a zero full first jet at one interior point would
force the solution to vanish throughout the connected open exterior.
    There can still, at this point, be constraint moment measures supported on S. Integrate the
smooth interior fields by parts in (7.11). Take K ′ = 0 and both form variations zero, and choose
a metric test with value and first jet zero at S. The trace of its normal second derivatives in the
tangential slots is arbitrary, so its scalar-curvature variation at S is arbitrary. Its area and expansion
variations, all integrated interior boundary terms, momentum variation and ray-transport terms at
S vanish. Equation (7.11) therefore kills the scalar boundary moment. Positivity and domination
of the vector moment by the scalar moment kill the vector boundary moment as well. Henceforth
the moments in that identity are just the smooth functions u, X times volume.
    The end constants and positive lapse. The closed first-jet system gives, for Y = (u, X) in end
coordinates,
                                   |D2 Y | ≤ Cr−1−q |DY | + Cr−2−q |Y |.                             (8.53)
Indeed curvature, ∇K, K 2 , the constraints and the electric stress multiply values, whereas K and
metric first derivatives multiply first derivatives. These orders require only the stated O2 /O1 tails.

                                                       71
Radial integration and Gronwall first give Y = O(r) and DY = O(1): write |Y (r)| as its initial
value plus the radial integral of |DY | and use the integrability of r−1−q . Equation (8.53) now gives
a limit of DY on each ray with error O(r−q ). The limits agree on all rays, since integration of D2 Y
along a path of length O(r) on a large sphere gives an O(r−q ) difference. Denote the common linear
part by A.
    The scalar affine part cannot be nonzero: u ≥ 0 on all large spheres. Once that part is zero,
|X| ≤ u rules out every growing vector affine part as well. Thus A = 0. Since n ≥ 4 and
q > (n − 2)/2, we have q > 1. Integration gives bounded Y , a common constant limit Y∞ , and
initially Y − Y∞ = O(r1−q ). Reinserting boundedness in (8.53) improves the Hessian to O(r−2−q ),
and two integrations give Y − Y∞ = O2 (r−q ). The stated weaker exponent q0 is therefore available.
    Apply (7.11) to the end prototypes and integrate by parts on large truncations. The interior
adjoint equations and the compact boundary terms cancel. Only the constant scalar multiplying
the linear scalar ADM flux, and twice the constant vector multiplying the linear momentum flux,
remain. All other boundary terms vanish by (8.53), the prototype orders and the background decay.
The surviving identity is

                  2kω b0 δEADM + bi δ(PADM )i = 2kω u∞ δEADM + X∞
                                                                i
                      (                           )        (                      )
                                                                  δ(PADM )i .                  (8.54)

The prototype fluxes span all entries, proving (8.46). If u vanished at an interior point, smooth
nonnegativity would give du = 0 there. Domination would give X = 0 and ∇X = 0 there as well,
since |X| ≤ u = O(d2 ). The full-jet uniqueness noted above would contradict u∞ = b0 > 0. Thus
u > 0 in the interior.
    The stationary Einstein equation. The stationary metric in (8.47) has second fundamental form
b = − sym ∇X/u = K on each slice. We recall the metric-adjoint computation, now with no interior
area source. Put Q(A, B) = A : B − (tr A)(tr B). Integration of the momentum pairing gives
                  (                           )       (                               )
                 u Rg − Q(K, K) + 2Q(K, b) = u Rg + Q(b, b) − Q(K − b, K − b) .                (8.55)

At b = K its metric first variation equals that of the stationary scalar action, at fixed lapse
and shift, up to the integrated divergence. Its spatial coefficient is −u Einij . Equation (7.20)
supplies the electromagnetic coefficient. The variation of the constraint volume factor vanishes by
complementarity, and isometric transport of the active unit-ball rays adds −(Jm )(i Xj) . Thus

                                      u(Ein −S)ij = −(Jm )(i Xj) .                             (8.56)

Gauss–Codazzi gives the remaining normal and mixed components as µm , Jm . Where µm = 0, DEC
gives Jm = 0. Where µm > 0, equality in

                                      −Jm (X) ≤ |Jm ||X| ≤ µm u

forces |X| = u and Jm = −µm X ♭ /u. Consequently all components combine to give (8.48), and the
extra matter can occur only at W = 0.
    Maxwell tests and a global potential. Test (7.11) with the metric and second tensor fixed and a
compactly supported closed variation of α. The resulting identity, after dividing by the nonzero
factor 4c2n , is
             ∫

              Ω

de Rham duality on the oriented open manifold int Ω therefore makes uE ♭ exact; see [3]. This step

                                                      72
does not presume simple connectivity. Define p by dp = (k − 1)uE ♭ . Since u is bounded at infinity

9    Electric transport and the complete vacuum static base
The stationary development supplied by the preceding section need not initially have a timelike
Killing field everywhere. We first transport the electric alignment from the boundary, and then
prove staticity without making any regularity assumption on the zero set of the Killing norm. The
charged case is reduced to vacuum by an explicit change of potential. In the zero-charge case,
a complete Riemannian circle extension excludes an additional interior zero set. No black-hole
uniqueness theorem is used in this section.
    Throughout this section, k = n − 1, c2n = k(k − 1)/2, and all spatial norms, divergences, and
volume forms without a subscript refer to the original metric g. Write Ω◦ = Ω \ S. We use
Proposition 8.9, whose hypotheses are the connected nonextremal equality hypotheses of the main
theorem. In particular, it supplies smooth functions and fields u, X, p on the original exterior, with

     u>0     on Ω◦ ,        u ≥ |X|,      dp = (k − 1)uE ♭ ,        d(iX α) = 0,          α = iE volg .    (9.1)

The potential tends to zero at infinity. On Rz × Ω◦ the stationary Lorentz metric and Faraday form
are
                g = −u2 dz 2 + gij (dxi + X i dz)(dxj + X j dz),   F = cn dz ∧ uE ♭ .         (9.2)
The Killing field is ξ = ∂z , and its future unit normal to a constant-z slice is n = (∂z − X)/u. Both
Maxwell equations hold. If G is the Einstein tensor and S the electromagnetic stress tensor with
the normalization fixed in the problem, the remaining matter tensor satisfies

                        G − S = µm u−2 ξ ♭ ⊗ ξ ♭ ,        µm = 0     where u > |X|.                        (9.3)

    Set                                                                (              )
                        2        2                            k−1       Q2
                 W = u − |X| = −g(ξ, ξ) ≥ 0,               κ=      1 − 2(k−1)              > 0.            (9.4)
                                                               2rh    r         h
The boundary conclusions of Proposition 8.9 are

                       X = uν,          ∂ν u + K(X, ν) = κ     on S,          W |S = 0.                    (9.5)

Either u > 0 on all of S, in which case dW = 2κX ♭ there, or u = 0 on all of S. In the latter case, in
the original distance s into the exterior from S,

                                     u = sa,    X = s2 Y,          a|S = κ,                                (9.6)

9.1   Boundary alignment and electric transport
Lemma 9.1 (Global electric alignment). The potential p is constant on S, and

                                       X ∧E =0      throughout Ω.                                  (9.9)

Proof. Suppose first that u > 0 on S. The stationary metric is smooth and nondegenerate up to
the hypersurface R × S. Equations (9.5) imply that ξ is a null normal to this hypersurface: it is
null, is tangent to it, and is orthogonal to all its tangent vectors. Its null second fundamental form
vanishes, since its flow preserves the metric induced on the transverse sections. The null expansion
equation consequently gives Ricg (ξ, ξ) = 0. This assertion only uses the one-sided smooth jets on
the hypersurface. The nonaffine normalization of its null generators contributes a multiple of the
expansion, which is zero.
    The extra tensor in (9.3) has zero contraction with (ξ, ξ) there. Maxwell null positivity therefore
gives
                                              0 = 2|iξ F |2g .
A covector annihilating a null vector has nonnegative Lorentz norm, and its norm is zero precisely
when it is proportional to that null vector’s metric dual. Thus iξ F annihilates the tangent space of
the horizon. Since iξ F = cn uE ♭ , the tangential component of E on S is zero. Equations (9.1) and
(9.5) give X ∧ E = 0 and dp|T S = 0. If u = 0 on S, these two assertions follow immediately from
X = 0 and dp = (k − 1)uE ♭ . Connectedness of S gives a single boundary value, denoted by pb .
    The divergence-free constraint and Cartan’s formula give

                           0 = d(iX α) = LX (iE volg ) = i[X,E]+(div X)E volg .

In particular,
                             [X, E] + (div X)E = 0,        LE (X ∧ E) = 0.                        (9.10)
Alignment therefore propagates along every nonzero electric trajectory once it is known at one point
of that trajectory.
     Here is the measure argument ensuring sufficiently many boundary hits. Let Π be a relatively
compact regular level patch p = p0 ̸= 0 in the interior. Follow V = sgn(p0 )E from this patch until
its first boundary hit, with maximal time T (y) ∈ (0, ∞]. Along each trajectory |p| increases strictly,
because
                                     dp(E) = (k − 1)u|E|2 > 0
where E ̸= 0. All these trajectories remain in a fixed compact subset of the original exterior: outside
a sufficiently large coordinate sphere, |p| < |p0 |. The trajectory map (t, y) ↦ Φt (y) is injective for
0 < t < T (y), by uniqueness of the flow and strict monotonicity of p.



                                                   75
    Let df be the positive transverse flux measure iV volg on Π, oriented appropriately. Since
div V = 0, the volume element in this flow tube is dt df. Consequently
                                  ∫
                                        T (y) df(y) ≤ Volg (KΠ ) < ∞,                           (9.11)
                                    Π

where KΠ is the fixed compact region just described. Hence T (y) < ∞ for almost every y in flux
measure. A finite-time trajectory cannot terminate in the interior of that compact region. This
follows by smoothly extending the vector field locally and using ordinary differential equation
existence. Nor can it reach a zero of E at a finite time, by uniqueness. It therefore hits S with
E ̸= 0.
    Equation (9.10) transports the boundary alignment back to almost every point of Π. The flux
density is smooth and strictly positive on this regular patch, so continuity gives alignment on the
whole patch. Varying the patch proves it at every point with p ̸= 0 and E ̸= 0. If p = 0 and E ̸= 0
at an interior point, the derivative of p along E is nonzero there, so such a point is a limit of the
preceding points. At zeros of E alignment is automatic. This proves (9.9).

9.3   The charged polynomial and the regular base attachment
Lemma 9.4 (A global polynomial when the charge is nonzero). If Q ̸= 0, then pb ̸= 0, 0 < |pb | < 1,
and
                                          p                          1 + p2b

Proof. The Maxwell equation in (9.1) gives

                                               div(u−1 ∇p) = 0.                                (9.30)

Its coefficients are smooth and elliptic on every compact subset of the interior. A positive maximum
above both boundary and end values, or a negative minimum below them, would be attained in such
a subset. The strong maximum principle therefore applies without requiring a uniform boundary
bound for u−1 . If pb = 0, it would give p = 0 and then E = 0, contrary to Q ̸= 0. Otherwise it gives
the stated strict interval for p.
    There is a useful flux which is smooth even at interior zeros of W :
                                                     1( i                  )

Indeed the axial eigenvalue of (u/W )C is 1/u, and its transverse eigenvalues are u/W ≥ 1/u. This

    The current j is bounded up to S. This is immediate when u > 0 there. In the other case

Thus P is constant, and its limit at infinity is zero. This proves the first equality in (9.29).
   Its root at pb is simple. Otherwise W = (p − pb )2 . If u > 0 on S, this contradicts dW = 2κX ♭ ̸= 0
there. If u = 0 on S, then dp = (k−1)uE ♭ gives p−pb = O(s2 ), whereas (9.6) gives W = κ2 s2 +O(s3 ),
again a contradiction. Writing p = tpb with 0 < t < 1, the polynomial becomes

                                        W = (1 − t)(1 − p2b t).

Every intermediate value is attained by continuity between the boundary and the end. Nonnegativity
implies p2b ≤ 1, and simplicity excludes equality. The displayed formula then makes W strictly
positive at every interior point.

Lemma 9.5 (Regular attachment of the orbit base). On each positive component adjacent to S,
the pair (hs , λ) has a smooth nondegenerate attachment at S. The attached boundary metric is g|T S ,
and
                                      λ|S = 0,     |dλ|hs |S = κ.                            (9.35)
If u > 0 on S, the attachment uses coordinates (λ, y) in place of original coordinates (W, y), and
                                                  1
                                         X♭ =       dW + W β                                    (9.36)
                                                 2κ
for a smooth one-form β in the original collar. In these attached coordinates hs is smooth and
invariant, as a tensor, under λ ↦ −λ. If u = 0 on S, the original smooth structure is retained, λ is
a smooth defining function, and As is smooth up to S.

Proof. In the positive-u case, W is an original boundary defining function. Equation (9.36) follows
from the boundary identity dW = 2κX ♭ and smooth division by this defining function. More
explicitly, write
                                                                          1

                                                    80
Upon setting W = λ2 , the metric components are

                                           (hs )λλ = 4λ2 gW W + 4b2 ,

                                          (hs )AB = gAB + λ2 βA βB ,

where every coefficient on the right is evaluated at (λ2 , y). Normal and tangential diagonal coefficients
are even and the mixed coefficients are odd, which is exactly tensor invariance under reflection. At
λ = 0 the normal coefficient is κ−2 , the mixed coefficients vanish, and the tangential metric is g|T S .
    In the zero-u case, put v = (a2 − s2 |Y |2 )1/2 in a small original collar. Then

                           λ = sv,       hs = g + s2 v −2 Y ♭ ⊗ Y ♭ ,   As = v −2 Y ♭ .

These expressions are smooth and nondegenerate, and v|S = κ. They prove the remaining assertions.

    Near the boundary the simple root in Lemma 9.4 makes p a smooth function of W = λ2 . Thus
