    Completeness of hs including its attachment follows from hs ≥ g. Indeed a finite-length Cauchy
escape has a limit in the complete original metric space. An interior limit is harmless because
W > 0 there, and a boundary limit is contained in the regular attached collar. The same reasoning
proves metric completeness, or equivalently the absence of any finite-length escape. Since

                                        1 − p2b ≤ 1 − pb p ≤ 1,

h0 and hs are uniformly comparable. Completeness passes to h0 . The attachment changes only
the boundary collar’s smooth defining function, so the topological end and compact-complement
properties are retained.




                                                    82
9.4   Zero charge and exclusion of an interior frontier
Proposition 9.8 (Global positivity when the charge vanishes). If Q = 0, then E = 0, p = 0, and
W > 0 on all of Ω◦ . The attached pair (hs , λ) is a complete vacuum static pair with 0 < λ < 1,
connected compact boundary, and boundary derivative κ.

Proof. The boundary value of p is constant by Lemma 9.1. Integrate div(pE) = dp(E) = (k−1)u|E|2
on the original exterior truncated at radius R. The boundary normal in this divergence theorem is
−ν on S. The outer term tends to zero by the decay of p and E, and the inner flux is −pb ωQ = 0.
Since u is bounded and |E|2 is integrable, this gives
                                                  ∫
                                        (k − 1)           u|E|2 = 0.
                                                      Ω

Positivity of u in the interior implies E = 0, and then p = 0 by its normalization at infinity.
   On every component of U, Lemmas 9.3 and 9.6 now give

                         Hesshs λ = λ Richs ,         ∆hs λ = 0,       Scalhs = 0.                (9.46)

Let C be the positive component containing the whole sufficiently distant end. Any other positive
component has compact closure in the original exterior and continuous boundary value λ = 0 on its
whole topological boundary, including any part on S. Its positive maximum would be attained in its
interior, contradicting the strong maximum principle. Thus no other positive component exists, and
all deleted boundary collars lie in C. The same compact-superlevel argument gives 0 < λ < 1 on C.
    We next exclude a finite orbit-metric distance to an interior zero. Near any such zero, u and |X|
are bounded below, and Lemma 9.3 makes the left side of (9.22) zero. Therefore

                                  (dT log W )i = |X|−2 X j (dX ♭ )ij .                            (9.47)

Its right side is bounded. Together with (9.18), this gives on C near the zero

                                                                (dW (e))2
                            |d log W |2hs = |dT log W |2g +               ≤ C.                    (9.48)
                                                                  u2 W
Consequently a finite hs -length curve cannot approach the zero: integration of (9.48) would keep
log W bounded on its tail.
    Attach S to the base of C as in Lemma 9.5. By (9.46), the Hessian of λ vanishes at S, and the
boundary is totally geodesic. In Gaussian base distance σ from S, smoothness gives the Taylor
expansions

                             hs = dσ 2 + γ0 + σ 2 γ2 + σ 3 R AB dy A dy B ,
                                          (                       )
                                                                                                  (9.49)
                                                  3        4
                              λ = κσ + a3 (y)σ + σ Rλ .                                           (9.50)

The remainders are smooth for σ ≥ 0. The absent quadratic term in the lapse is exactly
Hesshs λ(∂σ , ∂σ )|S = 0.
   Take a circle coordinate T of period 2π/κ, and put

                             hcirc = hs + λ2 dT 2         on C × (R/(2π/κ)Z).                     (9.51)

Collapse this circle at S by attaching a disk in the normal direction. The period is the same over
the entire connected boundary because κ is constant. To check regularity, set θ = κT , use disk


                                                      83
coordinates (x1 , x2 ) = (σ cos θ, σ sin θ), and write αD = x1 dx2 − x2 dx1 . Besides the Euclidean disk
metric, the polar contribution is
                           [(        )2     ]               [(        )2        ]
                                 λ                                λ                  2
                                                                                    αD
                                                2   2
                                          − 1 σ dθ =                       −1
                                κσ                               κσ                 σ2
                                                            2a3 (y) 2            2
                                                        =          αD + σB(σ, y)αD
                                                              κ
with smooth B. The first term has smooth quadratic coefficients in (x1 , x2 ); the remaining coefficients
have differentiated order at least three. The same holds for the remainder in (9.49). The extended
metric is therefore C 2,α for every 0 < α < 1. It is nondegenerate. The warped-product Ricci
formulas and (9.46) give Ricci curvature zero off the attached copy of S, and continuity extends this
identity across it. Harmonic-coordinate regularity for Einstein metrics then makes this extension
smooth [10].
    The resulting manifold, denoted by M, is complete and has no boundary. To check completeness
directly, a finite-length curve projects to a finite hs -length curve, hence to a g-Cauchy curve because
hs ≥ g. Completeness of the original metric space supplies a limit in Ω. An interior zero of W is
excluded by (9.48). At an interior point of C, the circle has uniformly positive size, so its coordinate
is Cauchy as well. At a point of S, the regular disk attachment makes all limiting circle phases
converge to that point of the attached zero section. Thus no finite-length escape remains, which
proves completeness.
    Suppose an interior frontier of C still existed. A sequence in C approaching such a frontier, lifted
at a fixed circle phase, escapes every compact subset of M. Choose a far coordinate sphere and its
product with the circle,
                                            ΣR ∼ = S n−1 × S 1 .
It is a compact connected cross-section. Its outside is the asymptotic product end, while its inside
contains the escaping sequence and is noncompact. The inside is connected: excursions of a path
through the outside can be replaced by paths in a thin inside collar of the connected cross-section.
A compact tubular neighborhood of ΣR therefore separates two noncompact regions. In particular
M has at least two ends.
    For clarity, the line needed for the splitting theorem can be constructed directly. Choose escaping
sequences in the two regions and minimizing segments between them. Each segment meets the fixed
compact separating cross-section, and its distances from that cross-section to both endpoints tend
to infinity. Centering there and taking a subsequence gives a complete minimizing line. The smooth,
complete, connected manifold M has Ric = 0, so the Cheeger–Gromoll splitting theorem applies
[8]. It gives M =  ∼ R × N with N complete and connected. Since the product with a connected
noncompact factor has only one end, N must be compact. Such a product has at most linear volume
growth.
    On the other hand, (9.8) shows that hs approaches a positive constant Euclidean metric and
λ → 1. The circle has fixed period. Hence the product end in (9.51) gives Volhcirc (BR ) ≥ cRn for
large R: paths to a fixed far sphere have bounded cost, and the metric on the remaining end is
uniformly comparable to its Euclidean product model. This contradicts linear growth. There is no
interior frontier. Since Ω◦ is connected, C = Ω◦ and W > 0 throughout.
    Now hs itself is complete up to its regular boundary by the same projection and collar argument,
and (9.46) holds globally. The boundary assertions follow from Lemma 9.5 and the vanishing
Hessian already proved.



                                                            84
Proposition 9.9 (Complete vacuum reduction). Under the stationary interface stated at the start
of this section, the Killing field is timelike on the whole open exterior: W > 0 on Ω◦ . The original
matter densities vanish there, X ∧ E = 0, and dAs = 0. The orbit pair (hs , λ) has the regular
attachment in Lemma 9.5.
     If Q ̸= 0, define (h0 , λ0 ) by (9.42); if Q = 0, take (h0 , λ0 ) = (hs , λ). In either case this is a
smooth vacuum static pair satisfying (9.43) on a connected orientable exterior, complete including
its connected compact boundary, with compact complement of exactly one Euclidean coordinate end.
Its lapse is zero at the boundary, lies strictly between zero and one in the interior, and tends to one
at infinity. The inward boundary derivative is a positive constant, equal to (9.44) in the charged case
and to κ in the uncharged case. The boundary is totally geodesic and Hessh0 λ0 = 0 there. Gaussian
reflection of h0 with odd reflection of λ0 is at least C 2,α for 0 < α < 1.
     After a fixed linear change of end coordinates,

                              h0 − δ = O2 (r−q0 ),        λ0 − 1 = O2 (r−q0 )                       (9.52)

for q0 as in (9.7). No simple-connectivity claim or global primitive of As is needed for this reduction.

Proof. All interior, boundary, and completeness assertions follow from Propositions 9.7 and 9.8,
together with Lemmas 9.1 and 9.3. On W > 0, (9.3) gives µm = 0, and the matter dominant energy
condition gives Jm = 0. Continuity gives the same conclusion up to S.
    In the original end coordinates, hs tends to δ + b♭ ⊗ b♭ , a positive constant matrix. Equations (9.8)
and (9.12) give differentiated error O2 (r−q0 ). In the charged case the additional conformal factor
differs from one by O2 (r2−n ), so the same bound holds for h0 and λ0 . A fixed linear coordinate
change normalizes the limiting matrix and preserves these decay orders.
    Finally take Gaussian normal coordinates for h0 at the boundary. Total geodesicity makes
the first normal derivative of its tangential metric zero. The vanishing boundary Hessian makes
the second normal derivative of the lapse zero. Even metric reflection and odd lapse reflection
consequently match their derivatives through order two and are C 2,α . This is the regularity required
for the vacuum doubling argument in the next section.


10     Vacuum doubling with a weak asymptotic tail
The vacuum pair obtained in Proposition 9.9 has only the asymptotic derivative bounds inherited
from the original data. We prove its uniqueness at that regularity. In particular, the point added in
conformal doubling will initially be a W 2,s point, rather than a smooth point. The nonnegative
mass statement used below follows from the numerical Theorem 2.5, which has already been proved;
no equality conclusion of that theorem is used.

Proposition 10.1 (Vacuum uniqueness). Let n ≥ 4 and let (B, h0 , λ0 ) be a smooth oriented vacuum
static exterior, complete including its connected compact boundary S, with a compact complement of
one Euclidean coordinate end. Suppose

                 Hessh0 λ0 = λ0 Rich0 ,       ∆h0 λ0 = 0,        λ0 |S = 0,     λ0 −→ 1,            (10.1)

and ∂ν λ0 = κ0 > 0 on S, with ν pointing toward the end. Assume, after a constant linear
normalization of end coordinates,
                                                                      n−2
                h0 − δ = O2 (r−q0 ),      λ0 − 1 = O2 (r−q0 ),            < q0 < n − 2.             (10.2)
                                                                       2


                                                     85
Then B is globally the exterior of a round sphere in isotropic coordinates. For some R > 0, writing
ρ = |x| ≥ R,
                               (           )4/(n−2)
                                   Rn−2                            1 − Rn−2 /ρn−2
                        h0 =    1 + n−2               δ,    λ0 =                  .                 (10.3)
                                   ρ                               1 + Rn−2 /ρn−2
The identification extends smoothly to S.

   We first give the end and analytic ingredients needed in its proof.

Lemma 10.2 (Harmonic coordinates and the missing metric monopole). Under the hypotheses of
Proposition 10.1, put
                                  2/(n−2)
                            ȟ = λ0       h0 , V0 = log λ0 .
There are harmonic end coordinates for ȟ, a number a0 > 0, and α > 0 such that, for every derivative
order j,
                    ȟij = δij + Oj (r2−n−α ),    V0 = −a0 r2−n + Oj (r2−n−α ).                (10.4)

Proof. The maximum principle gives 0 < λ0 < 1 in the interior. The conformal Ricci and Laplace
formulas applied to (10.1) give
                                          n−1
                                Ricȟ =       dV0 ⊗ dV0 ,       ∆ȟ V0 = 0.                         (10.5)
                                          n−2
We may decrease q0 while preserving its strict lower bound. Since n ≥ 4, it is possible to take q0 > 1;
hence δ0 = 1 − q0 lies in (2 − n, 0). This avoids a logarithmic coordinate correction.
     Here are details of constructing coordinates at the stated derivative count. Extend the coefficients
of ȟ to be Euclidean inside a large coordinate sphere, using a transition annulus. The equation for
y i = xi + v i is
                                     ∆δ v i = −∆ȟ xi + (∆δ − ∆ȟ )v i .
Use weighted scaled C 2,β norms of order δ0 , with a fixed 0 < β < 1. The Newton operator takes
order δ0 − 2 to order δ0 : splitting its integral into |z| < r/2, |z − x| < r/2, and the remaining exterior
annuli bounds the undifferentiated potential by C(1 + r)δ0 , and local scaled Schauder estimates
give its two derivatives and Hölder seminorm. Both integrals at infinity converge because δ0 < 0,
and δ0 > 2 − n controls the inner annuli. After choosing the cutoff radius sufficiently large, the
last operator has norm less than one. The Neumann series therefore gives v i = O2,β (r1−q0 ). The
original O2 bounds supply the scaled Hölder bounds for the first-order coefficients used here. The
new coordinate derivative is I + O(r−q0 ), so these are coordinates outside a larger sphere. There
is a derivative-count issue in transferring a metric to these coordinates: its second derivatives use
third derivatives of the coordinate correction. Differentiating the coordinate equation once, the
assumed O2 metric bounds control its right side in scaled Lt for every finite t. Local estimates
give v i in scaled W 3,t with the corresponding decay. Thus the transformed metric first has the
initial weighted W 2,t bounds for any finite t, which is enough for the next elliptic step. No third
asymptotic derivative of the original metric has been assumed.
    In these coordinates (10.5) is a uniformly elliptic system whose metric equation has principal
part −ȟab ∂a ∂b ȟij /2 and quadratic first-derivative terms. The equation for V0 has the same principal
coefficients. Scaled interior estimates first give C 2,β bounds and then, by differentiating the system,
all higher scaled bounds; see the elliptic estimates in [13]. Consequently

                        ∆δ (ȟij − δij ) = Oj (r−2−2q0 ),    ∆δ V0 = Oj (r−2−2q0 )



                                                      86
for every j. Cut the fields off inside a fixed sphere. Their Euclidean Laplacian sources are integrable
and have a finite positive moment of every order smaller than 2q0 − (n − 2). Choose

                                    0 < α < min{1, 2q0 − (n − 2)}.

Subtracting the Newton kernel monopole from the potential, the inner annuli are bounded by the α
moment and the outer annuli by the displayed pointwise source bounds. Thus

                ȟij − δij = Aij r2−n + Oj (r2−n−α ),         V0 = −a0 r2−n + Oj (r2−n−α ).

The difference from the Newton potential is an entire decaying harmonic function and vanishes.
Derivative estimates for the remainders follow by the same scaled interior estimates.
   Harmonic gauge now eliminates A. Its leading term is
                                                                   (                       )
         0 = ∂j ȟij − 12 ∂i ȟjj + O(|ȟ − δ| |∂ ȟ|) = (2 − n)r−n Aij xj − 12 (tr A)xi + o(r1−n ).

Hence A = (tr A)I/2, and taking the trace gives A = 0 because n ̸= 2. Finally the harmonic flux of
λ0 in h0 , with the outward domain normal −ν at S, gives

                                    (n − 2)ωa0 = κ0 Areah0 (S) > 0.                                    (10.6)

All changes between the Euclidean and metric fluxes have vanishing limit by the decay just
established.

   The next lemma specifies the inverse actually required at the point of compactification. Only
compact sources are used.

without boundary, and have one Euclidean coordinate end with compact complement. Suppose γ is
uniformly positive, locally W 2,s , and smooth off a compact set, where

                                                     87
Proof. Sobolev embedding gives continuous coefficients, first derivatives in Lp , and uniform ellipticity
on compact coordinate patches. In nondivergence notation ∆γ = aij ∂i ∂j + B i ∂i , the principal
coefficients are continuous and B ∈ Lp . Freeze the principal coefficients on a small ball. The
constant coefficient W 2,s estimate absorbs their small oscillation. For the first-order term use
                                                                   1  1 1
                            ∥B Dv∥Ls ≤ ∥B∥Lp ∥Dv∥Ln ,                = + .
                                                                   s  p n

Interpolation of ∥Dv∥Ln between the local W 2,s norm and a zeroth-order norm allows absorption,
with an additional zeroth-order constant. Cutoffs and a finite cover give the local W 2,s estimate.
These estimates are uniform for coefficients converging in W 2,s : their principal coefficients converge
uniformly, and the Lp first-order coefficients are uniformly equi-integrable. The same argument
gives estimates for smooth approximations and their Dirichlet problems.
    For large R, direct differentiation gives

                      ∆γ r−b = b(b + 2 − n)r−b−2 + O(r−b−2−q ) < 0            (r ≥ R).            (10.9)

Solve on expanding smooth truncations with zero outer Dirichlet value, initially using smooth
coefficient and source approximations. The maximum principle on the exterior region bounds the
solution there by its supremum on r = R times Cr−b ; local estimates also bound its scaled exterior
derivatives. The compact-core supremum is bounded by C∥f ∥Ls uniformly in the exhaustion and
approximations. Otherwise divide by that diverging supremum. The local estimates and the compact
embedding of W 2,s into C 0 give a nonzero limit harmonic on all of M , bounded by Cr−b at infinity.
The divergence-form strong maximum principle contradicts this limit. A maximizing point cannot
escape the fixed core because of the exterior barrier. Passing first in the coefficient and source
approximations on a truncation, and then through exhaustion, proves existence and (10.7). The
decaying homogeneous maximum principle proves uniqueness, including for sign-changing solutions.
Thus X is a Banach space isometric to LsK .
    For completeness, the perturbation estimate is an estimate between these particular spaces,
rather than an assertion about all weighted sources. The second-order coefficient difference is
bounded in C 0 by C∥γ ′ − γ∥W 2,s . The first-order difference is bounded in Lp by the same quantity,
while Dv ∈ Lp (K) by (10.7). Since p/2 > s, their product lies in Ls (K). Finally Rγ ′ v ∈ Ls because
v ∈ C 0 (K). This proves (10.8). All constants are for the fixed sets and reference metric; one chooses
the perturbation size only after those constants have been fixed.

Lemma 10.5 (Smooth nonnegative mass from the numerical theorem). Let (M, γ) be a smooth
connected oriented complete manifold without boundary, with one Euclidean coordinate end and
compact complement, scalar curvature zero, and finite ADM energy. Assume that γ − δ = Oj (r−q )
for every integer j ≥ 0, with q > (n − 2)/2. Then E(γ) ≥ 0.

Proof. Fix a smooth point o. The local Dirichlet Green function for −∆γ in a small ball has a
positive fundamental pole at o. Cut it off inside that ball. Its distributional Laplacian is −δo plus a
smooth compact source supported in an annulus away from o. Subtract the decaying solution of
that compact source given by Lemma 10.3. The result G is harmonic off o, tends to zero at infinity,
and has the same positive pole. The maximum principle on the region between a small sphere about
o and a large outer sphere gives G ≥ 0; the strong principle gives G > 0. In normal distance d from
o, the local fundamental solution estimates give, with c > 0,

                       G = c d2−n (1 + o(1)),        ∂d G = (2 − n)c d1−n (1 + o(1)),

uniformly in angle. These local estimates follow by rescaling the smooth elliptic coefficients in the
Green equation. At the retained end G = O2 (r−b ) for any 0 < b < n − 2, by Lemma 10.3 and
exterior estimates; its harmonic flux there is finite.
    For fixed ϵ > 0, set
                                γϵ = (1 + ϵG)4/(n−2) γ on M \ {o}.

This metric is scalar flat. On a sufficiently small sphere d = t, the normal toward the old end is the
increasing-d normal, and its conformal mean curvature has the sign of

                             n−2                        t       t
For this fixed ϵ, choose such a sphere and discard its inside. The remaining manifold is a connected
smooth exterior, complete including its compact boundary, with precisely the old coordinate end.
Give it second tensor and electric field zero. It has zero matter constraints, a strictly future
trapped boundary, and the required O2 metric decay with any fixed exponent between (n − 2)/2
and min{q, n − 2}. Its energy exists: choose b additionally so that b + q > n − 2, and use the finite
harmonic flux and the ADM calculation of Lemma 10.4.
   The numerical Theorem 2.5, at Q = 0, applies to this exterior. In particular it proves that its
energy is positive; timelikeness and a positive full-cut infimum are conclusions of that theorem, not
additional assumptions here. The conformal mass formula gives

Send ϵ ↓ 0, choosing the small inner sphere only after each ϵ is fixed. This proves the assertion. The
argument uses only the already established numerical theorem in dimension n and imposes no spin
assumption.

Lemma 10.6 (Rigidity of the compactified zero-mass metric). Let (M, γ) be oriented and satisfy
the hypotheses of Lemma 10.3, with Rγ = 0 weakly, E(γ) = 0, and q > (n − 2)/2. Suppose γ is
smooth except at one point and possibly along a smooth compact hypersurface where it is C 2,β . Then
(M, γ) is Euclidean space after passage to smooth harmonic coordinates.

Proof. Fix K containing all nonsmooth points. A finite coordinate partition, mollification, and a
cutoff within K give smooth positive metrics γj agreeing with γ off K and converging in W 2,s and
uniformly. Positivity follows from uniform convergence and the compact lower ellipticity bound. By
Lemma 10.4, they have positive conformal corrections Uj → 1. The corrected metrics are smooth by
elliptic regularity, complete because the factors are uniformly bounded above and below, scalar flat,
and have one retained coordinate end with decay exponent min{q, b} > (n − 2)/2. Their energies
converge to zero by (10.11) and are nonnegative by Lemma 10.5.
     More generally, let H be an arbitrary smooth symmetric tensor compactly supported in a smooth
patch of γ, and set γt = γ + tH for |t| sufficiently small. Enlarge K once to contain supp H, and
choose its reference inverse before varying t. Use this same Pγ , this same K, and (10.12) to obtain
Ut = 1 + vt and scalar-flat γ̂t . For each fixed t, approximate γt smoothly in W 2,s , keeping its exterior
fixed, and correct using that same reference inverse. The corrected smooth metrics converge to γ̂t ,
and their energies converge by (10.11). Consequently

                              E(γ̂t ) ≥ 0   for both signs of t,      E(γ̂0 ) = 0.

Only after taking the smoothing limit at each fixed t do we differentiate this two-sided inequality at
t = 0.
   Since Rγ = 0 and U0 = 1, differentiation of (10.11) gives
                 ⏐
            d ⏐⏐                 1                                   1
                                            ∫                                ∫
         0=      E(γ̂t ) = −                     Rγ′ [H] dVγ =                   ⟨Ricγ , H⟩ dVγ .   (10.14)
            dt 0
               ⏐             2(n − 1)ω       M                   2(n − 1)ω   M



                                                       90
The last identity follows by integrating R′ [H] = div div H − ∆ tr H − ⟨Ric, H⟩; the support lies in
a smooth interior patch, so no boundary terms occur. Thus Ricγ = 0 on every smooth patch. It
vanishes across the hypersurface by its C 2,β regularity as well. At the exceptional point, D2 γ ∈ Ls
and Dγ Dγ ∈ Lp/2 ⊂ Ls imply that distributional Ricci curvature has Ls coefficients. There is
therefore no point-supported curvature term, and Ricγ = 0 weakly throughout M .
     We detail the regularity upgrade, so that comparison geometry is applied to a smooth metric.
Fix n/2 < s < n and p = ns/(n − s) > n. In an original chart the scalar Laplacian has the form
aij ∂i ∂j + B i ∂i , where a is continuous, Da, B ∈ Lp , and DB ∈ Ls . Indeed DB consists of bounded
coefficients times D2 γ and quadratic Dγ terms in Lp/2 ⊂ Ls . Normalize a(x0 ) = I by a constant
linear change and rescale x = x0 + rz. The rescaled drift Br (z) = rB(x0 + rz) satisfies

              ∥a(x0 + r ·) − I∥∞ −→ 0,         ∥Br ∥Lp (B2 ) = r1−n/p ∥B∥Lp (B2r (x0 )) −→ 0.

Let GD be the flat Dirichlet inverse on B1 . On Dp = W 2,p (B1 ) ∩ W01,p (B1 ) the equations for
harmonic replacements y i = z i + v i are

                            v i = GD −Bri + (I − ar ) : D2 v i − Br · Dv i .
                                      [                                    ]


The operator involving v i has norm at most Cp (∥I − ar ∥∞ + ∥Br ∥p ), because ∥Dv∥∞ ≤ Cp ∥v∥W 2,p .
Choose r so this norm is less than 1/2. The Neumann series gives existence, uniqueness in Dp , and
∥v i ∥W 2,p ≤ Cp ∥Bri ∥p . A further decrease of r makes ∥Dy − I∥∞ < 1/2. Integrating along straight
segments in B1 makes y injective and bilipschitz, so it gives harmonic coordinates on a smaller ball.
     Approximate the metric smoothly in W 2,s on the rescaled ball. Then aj → a uniformly, Bj → B
in Lp , and DBj → DB in Ls . The same contraction gives harmonic coordinates yj → y strongly in
W 2,p and C 1 . For wj = ∂ℓ yj , differentiation gives

                       aj : D2 wj = −(∂ℓ aj ) : D2 yj − (∂ℓ Bj ) · Dyj − Bj · Dwj .

The right side is bounded in Ls : its first and last products are in Lp/2 ⊂ Ls , and its middle product
is in Ls . Local frozen-coefficient estimates give uniform W 2,s bounds for wj on smaller balls. Applied
to differences, these estimates and the already strong W 2,p convergence give strong local W 3,s
convergence of yj to y.
     The inverse chart x = y −1 is C 1 bilipschitz and W 2,p ∩ W 3,s . This follows by twice and three
times differentiating y(x) = id: the second derivatives of x are bounded factors times D2 y ◦ x,
and its third derivatives are bounded factors times D3 y ◦ x or (D2 y ◦ x)2 . The latter belongs to
Lp/2 ⊂ Ls . The transformed metric remains W 2,s for the same reason: its second derivatives contain
D2 γ and D3 x in Ls , and products of two factors among Dγ and D2 x in Lp/2 . On a fixed smaller
image chart the smooth transformed metrics converge strongly in W 2,s . Composition convergence
follows by approximation of the composed functions by smooth ones and the uniform Jacobian
bounds; the other factors converge in the displayed norms. Since Ricci curvature is continuous from
uniformly elliptic W 2,s metrics to Ls , the smooth Ricci transformation laws pass to this limit. Thus
the weakly Ricci-flat metric satisfies, in these harmonic coordinates,

                                      aab ∂a ∂b γij = Qij (γ −1 , Dγ),

where Q is quadratic in Dγ and belongs to Lt with t = p/2 > s.
   To obtain actual higher regularity from this equation, take a cutoff η supported in a small ball
and put u = ηγij . It has zero trace and satisfies

                      a : D2 u = ηQij + 2a(Dη, Dγij ) + (a : D2 η)γij =: F ∈ Lt ,

                                                    91
using Dγ ∈ Lp ⊂ Lt and boundedness of γ. Choose the ball so that, after normalizing a at its center,
the oscillation is small enough for the Dirichlet Neumann inverse of a : D2 at both exponents s and
t. The inverse at t gives a zero-trace solution v ∈ W 2,t of a : D2 v = F . Since t > s, both u and v
belong to W 2,s ; their difference has zero trace and solves the homogeneous equation. Uniqueness of
the same inverse at s gives u = v. Thus the metric actually gains W 2,t regularity on the smaller
ball, rather than merely satisfying a conditional higher-norm estimate.
    As long as s < n, the improved exponent is t = ns/[2(n − s)], so 1/t = 2/s − 2/n. Since s > n/2,
finitely many iterations reach an exponent greater than n, taking slightly smaller exponents if an
endpoint is met. The metric is then C 1,β , its quadratic source is C 0,β , and Schauder gives C 2,β
followed by smoothness. The classical harmonic-coordinate regularity theorem [10] is used only for
this final smooth continuation, after the initial Sobolev steps just proved.
    The resulting smooth metric is complete and Ricci flat. Its single Euclidean end gives asymptotic
volume ratio one: the compact part has finite volume and the metric error tends to zero, so distance
and volume comparison with exterior Euclidean annuli gives this limit for balls about any fixed point.
Bishop–Gromov comparison and its equality case [26] now apply. The volume ratio is nonincreasing,
has limit one both at radius zero and at infinity, and hence is identically one. The equality case
identifies the complete manifold with Rn .

Proof of Proposition 10.1. At S, (10.1) gives Hess λ0 = 0. Since ∂ν λ0 = κ0 > 0, the tangential
Hessian identity shows that S is totally geodesic. In Gaussian coordinates, reflect h0 evenly and
λ0 oddly. The vanishing first normal derivative of the tangential metric and the vanishing second
normal derivative of λ0 make these extensions C 2,β for 0 < β < 1. The static equations hold across
the seam by continuity. On the resulting two-ended double set

This is a nonempty interval. Estimate (10.16) gives W 2,s coefficients, since s(α′ − 2) + n > 0. They

    The filled double is connected, oriented, complete, without boundary, and has just the retained
coordinate end with compact complement. Completeness near the filled point follows from its
uniform ellipticity; it follows at the seam from its regular positive metric. It satisfies Lemma 10.6,
so it is Euclidean space.
    The seam is a connected compact embedded totally umbilic hypersurface in this Euclidean
space. Indeed its original second fundamental form is zero, and the conformal change (10.15) adds
a constant multiple of its induced metric, with nonzero coefficient because κ0 > 0. A connected
Euclidean totally umbilic hypersurface with nonzero principal curvature is contained in a sphere:
the Codazzi equation makes that curvature constant, and differentiation of its center x − ν/κEuc
gives zero. Compactness makes it the whole sphere. The retained component is its unbounded side.
After a translation denote the sphere radius by R.
    On that component h0 = U 4/(n−2) δ, with U = 2/(1 + λ0 ). Scalar flatness makes U Euclidean
harmonic, and it has boundary value two and limit one at infinity. The exterior Dirichlet maximum
principle identifies it uniquely as 1 + Rn−2 ρ2−n . This proves (10.3). The smoothness of the
identification at the seam follows either from its harmonic coordinates or from normal geodesic
collars of the two smooth metrics.


11     Recovery of the original data and sharp examples
The preceding uniqueness statement identifies the entire static base. We now use that global
identification to recover the original metric, second fundamental form, and signed normalized electric
field. The boundary analysis distinguishes a future horizon section from a bifurcation sphere. We
then verify the converse and examples within the purely electric class.

                                rhk−1 = m +       m2 − Q2 ,        m∗ = m.                     (11.11)

In particular rh is the outer simple root of f . Since r′ = λ > 0, the entire open base has range
rh < r < ∞. Equations (11.8) and (11.9) give dp = (k − 1)Qr−k dr. Substitution into (11.4) gives
precisely (11.2). The metric becomes (11.1).
   The graph map x ↦ (T, r, ϑ) = (−Φ(x), r(x), ϑ(x)) already recovers the original data on Ω◦ .
Indeed its pullback metric is hs − W A2s = g. In the stationary coordinates its future unit normal is

                                                    ∂z − X
                                              N=           .                                   (11.12)
                                                       u
The stationary equation sym ∇X = −uK gives the prescribed second fundamental form with this
normal. Directly contracting the first expression in (11.4) with (11.12) and restricting to z = 0
gives cn E ♭ . Its tangential restriction is zero. These identities also show why the global primitive
has recovered the original slice, rather than just a static replacement.
    It remains to verify the extension in regular horizon coordinates. The boundary surface gravity
from the stationary boundary law is
                                          (               )
                                  k−1      Q2                  1
                               κ=      1 − 2k−2               = f ′ (rh ) > 0.                 (11.13)
                                   2rh    rh                   2

Choose a tortoise primitive r∗ satisfying dr∗ /dr = f −1 . Near the simple outer root,

                                     r∗ = (2κ)−1 log W + b(W ),                                (11.14)

where b is smooth. The inversion from r to W = f (r) is smooth near the root.
    Suppose first u|S > 0. In the original smooth collar (W, y), the boundary laws and attachment
give
                       Xg♭ = (2κ)−1 dW + W β,       As = (2κ)−1 d log W + β,               (11.15)
with β smooth and closed on the whole original collar. The global function Ψ = Φ − (2κ)−1 log W
has differential β where W > 0. Fix w0 > 0 in the collar and define
                                                          ∫ W
                            Ψext (W, y) = Ψ(w0 , y) +           β(∂w )(w, y) dw.
                                                          w0

This is a global smooth function up to W = 0 and agrees with Ψ for W > 0. Closedness of β also
verifies its tangential derivatives, so no period or patching ambiguity occurs.
    The angular labels extend smoothly in the original collar as well. In the global radial coordinates
already obtained, the original orbit metric has the form hs = A1 (ρ) dρ2 + A2 (ρ)σk with positive

                                                     95
radial coefficients. Fixed-angle curves are therefore its normal geodesics after parametrization by
hs distance. This remains true after the radial conformal change from the vacuum base. In the
attached collar the map J(λ, y) = (−λ, y) is a smooth isometry of hs fixing S. Let π be its normal
geodesic projection to S. Uniqueness of normal geodesics gives π ◦ J = π. If ϑ0 : S → Sk is the
boundary angular diffeomorphism, ϑ0 ◦ π is a smooth J-invariant extension of the global radial
labels. Those labels are consequently smooth even functions of λ with smooth tangential parameters,
hence smooth functions of W = λ2 up to zero.
    On the original slice the ingoing coordinate

                                       v = T + r∗ = −Φ + r∗

is smooth by (11.14)–(11.15). In these coordinates the metric and field are

                   Gm,Q = −f dv 2 + 2 dv dr + r2 σk ,      Fm,Q = cn Qr−k dv ∧ dr.             (11.16)

This is the regular future horizon extension. The cancellation in T + r∗ fixes the future choice. The
boundary map has the form (v(y), rh , ϑ(y)), where ϑ : S → Sk is a diffeomorphism. It therefore
meets every future horizon generator exactly once. Since r − rh is a positive constant multiple of W
to first order, the collar map is an immersion.
    If instead u|S = 0, in an original smooth distance coordinate σ0 the boundary factorizations are

                                u = σ0 a,     X = σ02 Y,     a|S = κ.

Consequently λ is a smooth positive defining function and As = Xg♭ /W is smooth on the original collar.
The same fixed-level integral construction, now applied to As without a logarithmic subtraction,
extends its global primitive Φ smoothly to this collar. Hence T = −Φ is smooth there. Use Kruskal
coordinates
                                 U = −eκ(r∗ −T ) ,     V = eκ(r∗ +T ) .                       (11.17)
                    2
Since eκr∗ = λeκb(λ ) , both coordinates are smooth on the slice and vanish at S. Their normal
derivatives are nonzero, with opposite signs. They give a smooth immersion through the bifurcation
sphere; the round angular labels extend by the smooth base collar in this case.
    In both cases the induced metric of the extended immersion equals the original positive metric
by continuity. The unique future unit normal consequently extends smoothly, and so do the second
fundamental form and the Faraday contractions, with the identities already proved on the interior.
The interior graph is injective because its base projection is a diffeomorphism. At the boundary the
angular map is a diffeomorphism, and r = rh separates boundary points from interior points. The
collar descriptions prove immersion there. Moreover r is proper on the original closed exterior: it
tends to infinity on the sole coordinate end and the remaining core is compact. The inverse image
of any compact target set is therefore a closed subset of a compact r-sublevel set. Thus this is a
proper injective immersion and hence a global embedding including S.
    Finally the lapse and shift have controlled constant timelike limits at the original end. In the
static end coordinates the graph slope is uniformly bounded away from the null slope. More explicitly
λ2 |dΦ|2hs = |X|2g /u2 tends to |b|2 /(b0 )2 < 1. Integration along end rays gives |T | ≤ cr + o(r) + C
for some c < 1, after choosing the time origin. Thus the end approaches the corresponding spatial
infinity. The already vanishing matter constraints extend to S by smoothness, completing the
proof.

Figure 2: Boundary attachment and recovery of the original slice in the connected nonextremal
equality case. For positive boundary lapse, the upper left arrow is the squaring correspondence
from the attached collar to the original collar; its inverse is not smooth at the boundary. For zero

                                                 97
Proposition 11.2 (Converse and sharpness). Every model slice in the stated converse class, with
parameters M > |q0 | and actual invariant ADM mass and signed flux equal to M, q0 , attains equality.
For every such parameter pair and every n ≥ 4, this class contains the static exterior slice and
smooth compact radial time perturbations of it with K ̸≡ 0.

Proof. Let                                                 √
                                          k−1
                                         r+   =M+              M 2 − q02 .
The degenerate metric on the future horizon is r+  2 σ on its generator labels, independently of the
                                                      k
position along a generator. Thus a full section, including the bifurcation sphere, has area ωr+k . The

assumed lower bound for all full cuts, and the fact that the boundary is itself a full cut, give
                                                             k
                                        Amin = Areag (S) = ωr+ .

Using the actual mass and signed charge specified in the converse now gives the equality formula.
This argument does not identify the ADM parameters of arbitrary unqualified model slices.
   We verify the advertised examples, including their geometric hypotheses. Put

                                    f = 1 − 2M r−(k−1) + q02 r−2k+2 .

The static T = 0 slice has

                    g = f −1 dr2 + r2 σk ,                 E = q0 r−k f ∂r ,
                                                                         √
                                                 K = 0,                        r ≥ r+ .           (11.18)

The simple root makes the boundary a regular bifurcation sphere: in proper distance s ≥ 0,
r − r+ = f ′ (r+ )s2 /4 + O(s4 ). Thus g and E are smooth to the boundary and complete with it
included. The boundary is totally geodesic and future marginal.
    The ambient metric and field obey the stated Einstein–Maxwell normalization in every dimension.
For an explicit check, their time and radial mixed Ricci eigenvalues are
                                                 k ′
                                   − 12 f ′′ −      f = −(k − 1)2 q02 r−2k ,
                                                 2r
and their angular mixed eigenvalue is
                                 (k − 1)(1 − f ) − rf ′
                                                        = (k − 1)q02 r−2k .
                                          r2
With c2n = k(k − 1)/2, these are exactly Ricab = 2Fac Fb c − (Fcd F cd /k)Gab . The Faraday form is
closed, and its spacetime dual has constant sphere flux, hence is closed as well. Gauss–Codazzi
and these Maxwell equations give µm = Jm = 0 and div E = 0 on every spacelike slice with the
prescribed induced fields and vanishing induced magnetic two-form.
    For a nonstatic example choose a smooth nonconstant τ (r) compactly supported in an interval
r+ + δ < r < R, and use the graph T = τ (r). After multiplying τ by a sufficiently small constant it
is spacelike, since
                          gτ = a(r) dr2 + r2 σk ,  a = f −1 − f (τ ′ )2 > 0.                 (11.19)
Near the boundary and infinity this is exactly the static slice. Its magnetic two-form vanishes
identically, because
                               ι∗ (dT ∧ dr) = τ ′ (r) dr ∧ dr = 0.
Its normalized electric flux is q0 by Maxwell closure. For example its electric field is q0 r−k a−1/2 ∂r ,
as follows by contracting the Faraday form with its future unit normal.

                                                          98
    The end is unchanged. Its momentum is zero, and its energy is M : in asymptotic Cartesian
coordinates the metric perturbation is (f −1 − 1)ni nj , with f −1 − 1 = 2M r−(n−2) + O(r−2(n−2) ). The
ADM integrand has radial component (n − 1)(f −1 − 1)/r, giving precisely M with the normalization
[2(n − 1)ω]−1 . All required asymptotic derivative bounds and integrability statements follow.
    Every full cut has area at least ωr+   k . Indeed angular projection onto the boundary sphere has

differential whose k-dimensional Jacobian is at most (r+ /r)k ≤ 1 for the metric (11.19), regardless
of its positive radial coefficient. The integral of the pullback boundary volume form over an
end-oriented full cut is ωr+   k , by Stokes on the compact region between the cut and a large round

sphere. This remains valid for disconnected cuts and portions on the original boundary, with their
full intrinsic boundary counted. The comass bound for that pullback form proves the area lower
bound. The boundary attains it.
    There is also no smooth interior full weakly future trapped cut. For clarity its exclusion can be
checked directly for every spacelike radial graph above. Its outward unit normal within the slice is
a−1/2 (∂r + τ ′ ∂T ), while its future unit spacetime normal is
                                           f −1   fτ′
                                       N = √ ∂T + √ ∂r .
                                              a    a
The round sphere expansion is consequently
                                       k
                              θ+ (r) = √ (1 + f τ ′ ) > 0     (r > r+ ),                       (11.20)
                                      r a
because spacelikeness gives |f τ ′ | < 1. If an interior compact full cut were weakly future trapped,
take a point where its radial coordinate is maximal. The full exterior it bounds contains every
point with larger radius, so its end-oriented normal at that point is the outward radial normal. It is
tangent there to its enclosing round sphere from the inside. The graph mean-curvature comparison
gives Hcut ≥ Hsphere at contact, and their tangential traces of K agree because the tangent planes
agree. Equation (11.20) contradicts weak future trapping. This uses the full-cut condition to fix the
normal also when the cut is disconnected.
    Finally choose τ with τ ′ (r0 ) = 0 and τ ′′ (r0 ) ̸= 0 at some interior point. At that point the
radial second fundamental form component of the graph is Krr = f (r0 ) τ ′′ (r0 ) with the stipulated
                                                                     √

future normal convention. Thus K is nonzero. These examples satisfy all the connected-horizon
hypotheses, have the same area, energy, momentum and signed charge as the static examples, and
attain equality.
Completion of Theorems 2.7 and 2.8. Proposition 11.1 proves the Original-Data Rigidity Theo-
rem 2.7. Proposition 11.2 proves the Converse and Sharp Examples Theorem 2.8, including the
nonstatic purely electric examples.
